&#x20;**Christianity** is an [Abrahamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic) [monotheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheistic) [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion) based on the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible#Christian_Bible) and the teachings of [Jesus](1095706.md). The [Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospels) state that [Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Christianity) is the [Son of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_of_God_\(Christianity\)) and [rose from the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus) after [his crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus), whose coming as the [messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah#Christianity) ([Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_\(title\))) was [prophesied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament_messianic_prophecies_quoted_in_the_New_Testament) in the [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament) and [chronicled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronicle) in the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament). It is the [world's largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_religious_groups) and most widespread religion with over 2.3 billion followers, comprising around 28.8% of the [world population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_population). Its adherents, known as [Christians](18337522.md), are estimated to make up a majority of the population in [120 countries and territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_by_country).

Christianity remains [culturally diverse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_culture) in its [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity) and [Eastern branches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity), and doctrinally diverse concerning [justification and the nature of salvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justification_\(theology\)), [ecclesiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiology), [ordination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordination), and [Christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology). Most [Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_denominations), however, generally hold in common the belief that Jesus is [God the Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Son)[^cite_note-nontrinitarianism-9]—the [Logos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logos_\(Christianity\)) [incarnated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incarnation_\(Christianity\))—who [ministered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Jesus), [suffered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passion_of_Jesus), and [died on a cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus), but [rose from the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus) for the [salvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvation_in_Christianity) of humankind; this message is called [the gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_gospel), meaning the "good news". The four [canonical gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_gospels) of [Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew), [Mark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark), [Luke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke) and [John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_John) describe the [life of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_of_Jesus) and teachings as preserved in the early [Christian tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_tradition), with the Old Testament as the gospels' respected background.

Christianity [began in the 1st century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_1st_century), after the death of Jesus, as a [Judaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_Judaism) sect with [Hellenistic influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Judaism) in the [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) province of [Judaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaea_\(Roman_province\)). The [disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disciple_\(Christianity\)) of Jesus [spread their faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Christianity) around the [Eastern Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Mediterranean) area, despite [significant persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_in_the_Roman_Empire). The inclusion of [Gentiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentile) led Christianity to slowly [separate from Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split_of_Christianity_and_Judaism) in the 2nd century. Emperor [Constantine I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_I) decriminalized Christianity in the Roman Empire in 313 with the [Edict of Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Milan). In 325, Constantine convened the [Council of Nicaea](11118.md), where the [Nicene Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Creed) was adopted, and where Early Christianity was consolidated into what would become the state religion by around 380. The [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East) and [Oriental Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy) both split over differences in [Christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology) during the 5th century, while the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md) and the [Catholic Church](606848.md) separated in the [East–West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) in 1054. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) split into numerous denominations from the Catholic Church during the [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation) era (16th century). Following the [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery) (15th–17th century), Christianity expanded globally via [missionary work](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_mission), [evangelism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelism), immigration, and trade. Christianity played [a prominent role](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Role_of_Christianity_in_Western_society) in the [development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_ethics) of [Western civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture), particularly in [Europe](9239.md) from [late antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_late_antiquity) and the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_Middle_Ages).

The three main [branches of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination) are [Catholicism](606848.md) (1.3 billion people), [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) (800 million),[^cite_note-11] and [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy) (300 million), while other prominent branches include [Oriental Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches) (60 million) and [Restorationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restorationism) (35 million).[^cite_note-14] Smaller church communities number in the thousands. In Christianity, efforts toward unity ([ecumenism](146656.md)) are underway.[^cite_note-Briggs1975-15][^cite_note-Chia2018-16] In the [West](21208200.md), Christianity remains the dominant religion even with a [decline in adherence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_Christianity_in_the_Western_world), with about 70% of that population identifying as Christian. [Christianity is growing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_population_growth) in Africa and [Asia](689.md), the world's most populous continents. Many Christians are still [persecuted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians) in some regions of the world, particularly where they are a minority, such as in the [Middle East](19323.md), [North Africa](21714.md), [East Asia](19605700.md), and [South Asia](21566765.md).

## Etymology

Further information: [Christ (title)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_\(title\))

Early Jewish Christians referred to themselves as being of 'The Way' ([Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek_language): τῆς ὁδοῦ, romanized: *tês hodoû*), an expression possibly coming from [Isaiah 40\:3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaiah_40\:3), "prepare the way of the Lord".[^cite_note-22] According to [Acts 11\:26](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_11\:26), the term "Christian" (Χρῑστῐᾱνός, *Khrīstiānós*), meaning "followers of Christ" and referring to Jesus' [disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disciple_\(Christianity\)), was first used in the city of [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_centers_of_Christianity#Antioch) by the non-Jewish inhabitants there.[^cite_note-23] The earliest recorded use of the term "Christianity/Christianism" (Χρῑστῐᾱνισμός, *Khrīstiānismós*) was by [Ignatius of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch) around 100 [AD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anno_Domini).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElwellComfort2001266,_828-24] The name Jesus comes from [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language): Ἰησοῦς (*Iēsous*), probably from [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew)/[Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic): יֵשׁוּעַ (*Yēšūaʿ*)*.*

## History

Main article: [History of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Christianity).

### Early Christianity

Main article: [Early Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity)

Further information: [Historical background of the New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_background_of_the_New_Testament)

#### Apostolic Age

Main article: [Christianity in the 1st century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_1st_century)

Further information: [Chronology of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronology_of_Jesus)



The [Cenacle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cenacle) on [Mount Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Zion) in [Jerusalem](16043.md), claimed to be the location of the [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper) and [Pentecost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecost)

Christianity originated as a [Jewish movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements#Sects_in_the_Second_Temple_period) in [Judaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaea_\(Roman_province\)) during the 1st century AD, within the context of late [Second Temple Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_Judaism).[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 1 </sup>[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27] The earliest followers of Jesus were Jews who understood his life, death and expected return through familiar [Jewish apocalyptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_eschatology) and [messianic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism) frameworks. They remained embedded in [Jewish religious life](15624.md) and did not initially conceive of themselves as departing from Israel or founding a new faith. Following their conviction that Jesus had been [raised from the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus),[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 7 </sup> they established a community in [Jerusalem](16043.md) that awaited Jesus's [return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming) and eschatological renewal, seeking to spread this message throughout Israel and the [Jewish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora).[^cite_note-\:1-28]<sup>: 191 </sup>[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-32] Early Jewish Christianity maintained a high [Christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology) alongside monotheism.[^cite_note-33] This early community was led by the three [Pillars of the Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pillars_of_the_Church), namely [James the Just](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_the_Just), [Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter), and [John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Apostle).[^cite_note-34] What eventually became of this earliest [Christ-following community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Christianity) remains unclear; they were possibly displaced, relocated, or killed when the [Romans destroyed Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\)) in 70 AD.[^cite_note-\:1-28]<sup>: 190 </sup>

Meanwhile, as the message about Jesus spread beyond [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea) and [Galilee](12639.md) into the broader [Greco-Roman world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman_world), it increasingly attracted [Gentile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentile) adherents, especially among "[God-fearers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God-fearer)": non-Jews who were attracted to Judaism and participated in [synagogal worship](48559.md) without fully [converting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Judaism).[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 8–9 </sup> This created a challenge for the movement's [Jewish religious outlook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_principles_of_faith), which insisted on close observance of the Jewish commandments. Against this backdrop, [Paul the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle), a Jew of the [Pharisaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharisees) school[^cite_note-\:1-28]<sup>: 42 </sup> who had initially persecuted the Jesus movement, became one of its most influential missionaries following his conversion, focusing on spreading the message to non-Jews in the [Eastern Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Mediterranean).[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 2, 8–9 </sup> Paul argued that Gentiles could participate in the promises of Israel through exclusive devotion to the [God of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism), [faith in Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pistis_Christou), [baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism), and [participation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Participation_in_Christ) in his death and resurrection, without full adherence to the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah), including [circumcision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumcision_controversy_in_early_Christianity).[^cite_note-35] This had a formative effect on the emerging [Christian](18337522.md) identity as [separate from Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split_of_Christianity_and_Judaism). Over time, such developments contributed to the gradual differentiation between the Jesus movement and other Jewish communities, a process that resulted in the emergence of Christianity as a separate religion.[^cite_note-36]

#### Ante-Nicene period

Main article: [Christianity in the ante-Nicene period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_ante-Nicene_period)

Further information: [Great Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Church) and [Gnosticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnosticism)



A folio from [Papyrus 46](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papyrus_46), an early-3rd-century collection of [Pauline epistles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_epistles)

This formative period was followed by the early [bishops](4092.md), whom Christians consider the [successors of Christ's apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_succession). From the year 150, Christian teachers began to produce theological and apologetic works aimed at defending the faith. These authors are known as the [Church Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Fathers), and the study of them is called [patristics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patristics). Notable early Fathers include [Ignatius of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch), [Polycarp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polycarp), [Justin Martyr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Martyr), [Irenaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irenaeus), [Tertullian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertullian), [Clement of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_of_Alexandria) and [Origen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origen).

During this period, there was no unified Christian movement but rather [multiple competing sects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diversity_in_early_Christian_theology), which held radically different beliefs. Christian authors began using the Greek term *hairesis*, meaning "school of thought", to describe rival communities, which gave rise to the concept of [heresy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heresy_in_Christianity).[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 32–33 </sup> In the second and third centuries, figures like [Justin Martyr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Martyr), [Irenaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irenaeus), [Tertullian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertullian), and [Clement of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_of_Alexandria), often retrospectively referred to as "[proto-orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-orthodox_Christianity)", insisting that there was one original apostolic teaching and that all rivals had deviated from it.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 33 </sup> Meanwhile, [Valentinus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valentinus_\(Gnostic\)), an [Alexandrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) teacher, developed a [Gnostic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnosticism) theology in which Christ brought saving knowledge (*[gnosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnosis)*) that enabled believers to be redeemed from the material world.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 39–40 </sup> [Marcion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcion_of_Sinope), from [Sinope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinop,_Turkey), drew a distinction between high, purely good god, father of Christ, and the lower, merely "just" god of Israel, who demanded [sacrifice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korban) and [procreation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be_fruitful_and_multiply).[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 41–42 </sup> Marcion is also known to have assembled a collection of Christian texts, including his own [gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Marcion), and in doing so created one of the earliest known Christian scriptural canons.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 43 </sup> Justin Martyr, from [Neapolis](233369.md), disagreed with both, arguing the God of the Jewish scriptures was Christ himself before his incarnation.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 45 </sup> A gradual splitting off of [Gentile Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentile_Christianity) left [Jewish Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Christians) continuing to follow the [Law of Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Moses), including practices such as circumcision. By the fifth century, they and the [Jewish–Christian gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish%E2%80%93Christian_gospels) would be largely suppressed by the dominant sects in both Judaism and Christianity.

Christianity occupied an unstable position within the Roman world. In antiquity, what moderns call "religion" was closely associated with ancestral traditions and embedded within other aspects of ethnic identity.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 61 </sup> It is possible that Gentile Christians, having rejected their native gods, were perceived by their pagan neighbors as disrupting the relationship between the divine and the community, on which civic welfare depended.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 65–66 </sup> Early examples of [persecution of Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians) include the [Acts of the Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles), which portrays [Saint Stephen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Stephen) as being killed by a mob in Jerusalem, while Paul writes that he himself had persecuted Christians prior to his vision of Christ.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 64–66 </sup> Additionally, [Tacitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacitus) wrote that Emperor [Nero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nero) blamed Christians for the [Great Fire of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Fire_of_Rome) in 64 AD.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 67–68 </sup> In the following centuries, Church Fathers recorded accusations that Christians were responsible for droughts, famines, and plagues.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 74 </sup> Nevertheless, before the mid-3rd century, persecutions were often reactive, sporadic and local.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 73 </sup> This changed in 249, when the emperor [Decius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decius), who ruled the empire following a long period of instability, ordered all residents of the empire (except Jews) to perform a [sacrifice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrifice_in_ancient_Roman_religion) to the [Roman gods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_mythology).[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 76 </sup> Jews had long been excused on the basis of ancestral custom in ways that Christians, lacking any such established tradition, were not.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 65 </sup> The [Diocletianic Persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocletianic_Persecution) beginning in 303 was particularly severe; Christians were accused of attracting demons that disrupted traditional cult practices and threatened public order, and were subjected to confiscations, destruction of buildings, expulsions, imprisonment, and demands for sacrifice.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 79–81 </sup> The situation changed under [Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great) (r. 306–337), who granted imperial support to Christianity, though under his rule and that of his successors, forms deemed heretical were suppressed.[^cite_note-\:0-25]<sup>: 202–204 </sup>

### Spread in the Roman Empire

Further information: [Christianity in late antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_late_antiquity)

See also: [Historiography of the Christianization of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_of_the_Christianization_of_the_Roman_Empire)



The [Monastery of St. Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar_Mattai_Monastery), located atop [Mount Alfaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Alfaf) in northern [Iraq](7515928.md), is recognized as one of the oldest Christian monasteries in existence.

[^cite_note-37]

Christianity spread to [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic)-speaking peoples along the [Mediterranean coast](19006.md) and also to the inland parts of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) and beyond that into the [Parthian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire) and the later [Sasanian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire), including [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), which was dominated at different times and to varying extents by these empires.[^cite_note-38] The presence of Christianity in Africa began in the middle of the 1st century in Egypt and by the end of the 2nd century in the region around [Carthage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthage). [Mark the Evangelist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_the_Evangelist) is claimed to have started the [Church of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Alexandria) in about 43 AD; various later churches claim this as their own legacy, including the [Coptic Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40][^cite_note-41] Important Africans who influenced the early development of Christianity include [Tertullian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertullian), [Clement of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_of_Alexandria), [Origen of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origen_of_Alexandria), [Cyprian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyprian), [Athanasius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athanasius), and [Augustine of Hippo](2030.md).



The 7th-century [Khor Virap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khor_Virap) monastery in the shadow of [Mount Ararat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Ararat); [Armenia](10918072.md) was the first state to adopt Christianity as the state religion in the early 4th century AD.

[^cite_note-CIA-42]

[^cite_note-almanac-43]

[King Tiridates III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiridates_III_of_Armenia) made Christianity the [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion) in [Armenia](10918072.md) in the early 4th century, making Armenia the first officially Christian state.[^cite_note-CIA-42][^cite_note-almanac-43] It was not an entirely new religion in Armenia, having penetrated into the country from at least the third century, but it may have been present even earlier.[^cite_note-44]

[Constantine I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great) was exposed to Christianity in his youth, and throughout his life his support for the religion grew, culminating in baptism on his deathbed.[^cite_note-45] During his reign, state-sanctioned persecution of Christians was ended with the [Edict of Toleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Serdica) in 311 and the [Edict of Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Milan) in 313. At that point, Christianity was still a minority belief, comprising perhaps only 5% of the Roman population.[^cite_note-46] Influenced by his adviser [Mardonius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardonius_\(philosopher\)), Constantine's nephew [Julian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_\(emperor\)) unsuccessfully tried to suppress Christianity.[^cite_note-47] On 27 February 380, [Theodosius I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodosius_I), [Gratian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gratian), and [Valentinian II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valentinian_II) issued the [Edict of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Thessalonica) establishing [Nicene Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Christianity) as the [State church of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_church_of_the_Roman_Empire).[^cite_note-48] As soon as it became connected to the state, Christianity grew wealthy; the Church solicited donations from the rich and could now own land.[^cite_note-49]

Constantine was also instrumental in the convocation of the [First Council of Nicaea](11118.md) in 325, which sought to address [Arianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arianism) and formulated the Nicene Creed, which is still used by in [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism), [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy), [Lutheranism](23371382.md), [Anglicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism), and many other [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) churches.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-UMC—Our_Common_Heritage_as_Christians-51] Nicaea was the first of a series of [ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_council), which formally defined critical elements of the theology of the Church, notably concerning [Christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology).[^cite_note-52] The [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East) did not accept the third and following ecumenical councils and is still separate today by its successors ([Assyrian Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Church_of_the_East)).[^cite_note-53]

In terms of prosperity and cultural life, the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) was one of the peaks in [Christian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_history) and [Christian civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_civilization),[^cite_note-Cameron_2006_42-54] and [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) remained the leading city of the [Christian world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_world) in size, wealth, and culture.[^cite_note-55] [There was a renewed interest in classical Greek philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_scholars_in_the_Renaissance), as well as an increase in literary output in vernacular Greek.[^cite_note-56] Byzantine art and literature held a preeminent place in Europe, and the cultural impact of [Byzantine art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_art) on the West during this period was enormous and of long-lasting significance.[^cite_note-57] The later rise of [Islam](6037917.md) in North Africa reduced the size and numbers of Christian congregations, leaving in large numbers only the [Coptic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church) in Egypt, the [Ethiopian Orthodox Tewahedo Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Orthodox_Tewahedo_Church) in the Horn of Africa and the [Nubian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Sudan) in the Sudan (Nobatia, Makuria and Alodia).

### Middle Ages

Main article: [Christianity in the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_Middle_Ages)

#### Early Middle Ages

With the decline and [fall of the Roman Empire in the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire), the [papacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papacy) became a political player, first visible in [Pope Leo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Leo_I)'s diplomatic dealings with [Huns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attila_the_Hun) and [Vandals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandals).[^cite_note-Gonzalez-p238-58] The church also entered into a long period of missionary activity and expansion among the various tribes. While [Arianists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arianism) instituted the death penalty for practicing pagans (see the [Massacre of Verden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre_of_Verden), for example), [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism) also spread among the [Hungarians](17106358.md), the [Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples),[^cite_note-Gonzalez-p238-58] the [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celts), the [Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_peoples) and some [Slavic peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_peoples).[^cite_note-59]

Around 500, Christianity was thoroughly integrated into Byzantine and [Kingdom of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostrogothic_Kingdom) culture[^cite_note-60] and [Benedict of Nursia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedict_of_Nursia) set out [his Monastic Rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_of_Saint_Benedict), establishing a system of regulations for the foundation and running of [monasteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasteries).[^cite_note-Gonzalez-p238-58] [Monasticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasticism) became a powerful force throughout Europe,[^cite_note-Gonzalez-p238-58] and gave rise to many early centers of learning, most famously in [Ireland](147575.md), [Scotland](26994.md), and [Gaul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaul), contributing to the [Carolingian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolingian_Renaissance) of the 9th century.



[Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom) by AD 600 after its [spread to Africa and Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Christianity) from the Middle East

In the 7th century, [Muslims conquered Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Syria) (including [Jerusalem](16043.md)), North Africa, and Spain, converting some of the Christian population to [Islam](6037917.md), including some of the [Christian populations in pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia), and placing the rest under a separate [legal status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi).[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-Cahen-62] Part of the Muslims' success was due to the exhaustion of the Byzantine Empire in its decades long conflict with [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMullin200888-63] Beginning in the 8th century, with the rise of [Carolingian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolingian) leaders, the Papacy sought greater political support in the [Frankish Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankish_Kingdom).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMullin200893–94-64]

The Middle Ages brought about major changes within the church.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] [Pope Gregory the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Gregory_the_Great) dramatically reformed the [ecclesiastical structure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastical_polity) and administration.[^cite_note-69] In the early 8th century, [iconoclasm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iconoclasm) became a divisive issue, when it was sponsored by the [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium) emperors. The [Second Ecumenical Council of Nicaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Council_of_Nicaea) (787) finally pronounced in favor of icons.[^cite_note-70] In the early 10th century, Western Christian monasticism was further rejuvenated through the leadership of the great Benedictine monastery of [Cluny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cluny_Abbey).[^cite_note-71]

#### High and Late Middle Ages



An example of Byzantine pictorial art, the [Deësis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De%C3%ABsis) mosaic at the [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia#De%C3%ABsis_mosaic) in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople)



[Pope Urban II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Urban_II) at the [Council of Clermont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Clermont), where he preached the [First Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Crusade). Illustration by [Jean Colombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Colombe) from the *[Passages d'outremer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passages_d'outremer)*, c. 1490.

In the West, from the 11th century onward, some older cathedral schools [became universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_university) (see, for example, [University of Oxford](31797.md), [University of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Paris) and [University of Bologna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bologna)). Previously, higher education had been the domain of Christian [cathedral schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_school) or [monastic schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monastic_school) (*Scholae monasticae*), led by [monks](419369.md) and [nuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nun). Evidence of such schools dates back to the 6th century AD.[^cite_note-72] These new universities expanded the curriculum to include academic programs for clerics, lawyers, civil servants, and physicians.[^cite_note-73] The university is generally regarded as an institution that has its origin in the [Medieval Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity) setting.[^cite_note-verger1999-74][^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76]

Accompanying the rise of the "new towns" throughout Europe, [mendicant orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mendicant_order) were founded, bringing the [consecrated religious life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consecrated_life_\(Catholic_Church\)) out of the monastery and into the new urban setting. The two principal mendicant movements were the [Franciscans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franciscans)[^cite_note-77] and the [Dominicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Order),[^cite_note-78] founded by [Francis of Assisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi) and [Dominic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Dominic), respectively. Both orders made significant contributions to the development of the great universities of Europe. Another new order was the [Cistercians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cistercians), whose large, isolated monasteries spearheaded the settlement of former wilderness areas. In this period, church building and ecclesiastical architecture reached new heights, culminating in the orders of [Romanesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanesque_architecture) and [Gothic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture) and the building of the great European cathedrals.[^cite_note-79]

[Christian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_nationalism) emerged during this era in which Christians felt the desire to recover lands in which Christianity had historically flourished.[^cite_note-80] From 1095 under the pontificate of [Urban II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_II), the [First Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Crusade) was launched.[^cite_note-81] These were a series of military campaigns in the [Holy Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land) and elsewhere, initiated in response to pleas from the Byzantine Emperor [Alexios I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexios_I) for aid against [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_people) expansion. The Crusades ultimately failed to stifle Islamic aggression and even contributed to Christian enmity with the sacking of [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) during the [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade).[^cite_note-82]

The Christian Church experienced internal conflict between the 7th and 13th centuries that resulted in a [schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East-West_Schism) between the [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church) of [Western Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity) branch, the now-Catholic Church, and an [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity), largely Greek, branch (the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md)). The two sides disagreed on a number of administrative, liturgical and doctrinal issues, most prominently [Eastern Orthodox opposition to papal supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_opposition_to_papal_supremacy).[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] The [Second Council of Lyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Council_of_Lyon) (1274) and the [Council of Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Florence) (1439) attempted to reunite the churches, but in both cases, the Eastern Orthodox refused to implement the decisions, and the two principal churches remain in schism to the present day. However, the Catholic Church has achieved union with various [smaller eastern churches](214903.md).

In the thirteenth century, a new emphasis on Jesus' suffering, exemplified by the Franciscans' preaching, had the consequence of turning worshippers' attention towards Jews, on whom [Christians had placed the blame for Jesus' death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_deicide). Christianity's limited tolerance of Jews was not new—Augustine of Hippo said that Jews should not be allowed to enjoy the citizenship that Christians took for granted—but the growing antipathy towards Jews was a factor that led to [the expulsion of Jews from England in 1290](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Expulsion), the first of many such expulsions in Europe.[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86]

Beginning around 1184, following the crusade against [Cathar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathars) heresy,[^cite_note-87] various institutions, broadly referred to as the [Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquisition), were established with the aim of suppressing [heresy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heresy) and securing religious and doctrinal unity within Christianity through [conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conversion) and prosecution.[^cite_note-88]

### Modern era

Main article: [Christianity in the modern era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_modern_era)

#### Reformation and response

Main articles: [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation) and [Counter-Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-Reformation)

See also: [European wars of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_wars_of_religion) and [Renaissance Papacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_Papacy)



[Martin Luther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther) initiated the [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation) with his *[Ninety-five Theses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninety-five_Theses)* in 1517.

The 15th-century [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) brought about a renewed interest in ancient and classical learning. During the [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation), [Martin Luther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther) posted the *[Ninety-five Theses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninety-five_Theses)* 1517 against the sale of [indulgences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indulgences).[^cite_note-Simon-89] Printed copies soon spread throughout Europe. In 1521 the [Edict of Worms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Worms) condemned and excommunicated Luther and his followers, resulting in the schism of the [Western Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity) into several branches.[^cite_note-90]

Other reformers like [Zwingli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huldrych_Zwingli), [Oecolampadius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Oecolampadius), [Calvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Calvin), [Knox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Knox), and [Arminius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobus_Arminius) further criticized Catholic teaching and worship. These challenges developed into the movement called [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism), which repudiated the [primacy of the pope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_primacy), the role of tradition, the [seven sacraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_sacraments), and other doctrines and practices.[^cite_note-Simon-89] The [Reformation in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Reformation) began in 1534, when King [Henry VIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_VIII) had himself [declared head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Supremacy) of the [Church of England](5955.md). Beginning in 1536, the monasteries throughout England, Wales and Ireland were [dissolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_monasteries).[^cite_note-91]

[Thomas Müntzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_M%C3%BCntzer), [Andreas Karlstadt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Karlstadt) and other theologians perceived both the Catholic Church and the confessions of the [Magisterial Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magisterial_Reformation) as corrupted. Their activity brought about the [Radical Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_Reformation), which gave birth to various [Anabaptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anabaptism) denominations.



[Michelangelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelangelo)'s 1498–99 *[Pietà](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelangelo's_Piet%C3%A0)* in [St. Peter's Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Peter's_Basilica); the Catholic Church was among the patronages of the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance).

[^cite_note-92]

[^cite_note-93]

[^cite_note-94]

Partly in response to the Protestant Reformation, the Catholic Church engaged in a substantial process of reform and renewal, known as the [Counter-Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-Reformation) or Catholic Reform.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004242–244-95] The [Council of Trent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Trent) clarified and reasserted Catholic doctrine. During the following centuries, competition between Catholicism and Protestantism became deeply entangled with political struggles among European states.[^cite_note-96]

Meanwhile, the discovery of America by [Christopher Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus) in 1492 brought about a new wave of missionary activity. Partly from missionary zeal, but under the impetus of [colonial expansion](7299.md) by the European powers, Christianity spread to the Americas, Oceania, East Asia and sub-Saharan Africa.

Throughout Europe, the division caused by the Reformation led to outbreaks of [religious violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_violence) and the establishment of separate state churches in Europe. [Lutheranism](23371382.md) spread into the northern, central, and eastern parts of present-day Germany, [Livonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonia), and Scandinavia. [Anglicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism) was established in England in 1534. [Calvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinism) and its varieties, such as [Presbyterianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterianism), were introduced in Scotland, the Netherlands, Hungary, Switzerland, and France. [Arminianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arminianism) gained followers in the Netherlands and [Frisia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frisia). Ultimately, these differences led to the outbreak of [conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_war) in which religion played a key factor. The [Thirty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years'_War), the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War), and the [French Wars of Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Wars_of_Religion) are prominent examples. These events intensified the [Christian debate on persecution and toleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_debate_on_persecution_and_toleration).[^cite_note-97]

In the revival of neoplatonism [Renaissance humanists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_humanism) did not reject Christianity; quite the contrary, many of the greatest works of the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) were devoted to it, and the Catholic Church patronized many works of [Renaissance art](311401.md).[^cite_note-openuni-98] Much, if not most, of the new art was commissioned by or in dedication to the Church.[^cite_note-openuni-98] Some scholars and historians attribute Christianity to having contributed to the rise of the [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution).[^cite_note-99] Many well-known historical figures who influenced [Western science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_science) considered themselves Christian such as [Nicolaus Copernicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolaus_Copernicus),[^cite_note-100] [Galileo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei),[^cite_note-101] [Johannes Kepler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Kepler),[^cite_note-102] [Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton)[^cite_note-103] and [Robert Boyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Boyle).[^cite_note-104]

#### Post-Enlightenment



A depiction of [Madonna and Child](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madonna_and_Child) in a 19th-century [Kakure Kirishitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakure_Kirishitan) [Japanese woodcut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukiyo-e)

In the era known as the [Great Divergence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Divergence), when in the West, the [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) and the [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution) brought about great societal changes, Christianity was confronted with various forms of [skepticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skepticism) and with certain modern [political ideologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology), such as versions of [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism) and [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism).[^cite_note-105] Events ranged from mere [anti-clericalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-clericalism) to violent outbursts against Christianity, such as the [dechristianization of France during the French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dechristianisation_of_France_during_the_French_Revolution),[^cite_note-106] the [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War), and certain [Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism) movements, especially [the Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution_\(1917\)) and the [persecution of Christians in the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_in_the_Soviet_Union) under [state atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_atheism).[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110]

Especially pressing in Europe was the formation of [nation states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_states) after the [Napoleonic era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_era). In all European countries, different Christian denominations found themselves in competition to greater or lesser extents with each other and with the state. Variables were the relative sizes of the denominations and the religious, political, and ideological orientation of the states. Urs Altermatt of the [University of Fribourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Fribourg), looking specifically at Catholicism in Europe, identifies four models for the European nations. In traditionally Catholic-majority countries such as Belgium, Spain, and Austria, to some extent, religious and national communities are more or less identical. Cultural symbiosis and separation are found in Poland, the Republic of Ireland, and Switzerland, all countries with competing denominations. Competition is found in Germany, the Netherlands, and again Switzerland, all countries with minority Catholic populations, which to a greater or lesser extent identified with the nation. Finally, separation between religion (again, specifically Catholicism) and the state is found to a great degree in France and Italy, countries where the state actively opposed itself to the authority of the Catholic Church.[^cite_note-111]

The combined factors of the formation of nation states and [ultramontanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultramontanism), especially in Germany and the Netherlands, but also in England to a much lesser extent,[^cite_note-112] often forced Catholic churches, organizations, and believers to choose between the national demands of the state and the authority of the Church, specifically the papacy. This conflict came to a head in the [First Vatican Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Vatican_Council), and in Germany would lead directly to the *[Kulturkampf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kulturkampf)*.[^cite_note-113]



[Ordination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordination) of new pastors in [Cameroon](5447.md), 2014

Christian commitment in Europe dropped as modernity and secularism came into their own,[^cite_note-114] particularly in the [Czech Republic](5321.md) and [Estonia](28222445.md),[^cite_note-115] while religious commitments in America have been generally high in comparison to Europe. Changes in worldwide Christianity over the last century have been significant, since 1900, Christianity has spread rapidly in the [Global South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_South) and Third World countries.[^cite_note-116] The late 20th century has shown the shift of Christian adherence to the [Third World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_World) and the Southern Hemisphere in general,[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118] with the West no longer the chief standard bearer of Christianity. Approximately 7 to 10% of [Arabs](2185.md) are [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians_and_Arabic-speaking_Christians),[^cite_note-119] most prevalent in Egypt, [Syria](7515849.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md).[^cite_note-120]

## Beliefs

While Christians worldwide share basic convictions, there are differences of interpretations and opinions of the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) and [sacred traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_tradition) on which Christianity is based.[^cite_note-121] Christianity encompasses various movements of [Christian theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_theology), the main ones being conservative [fundamentalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_fundamentalism) or [moderate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderate_Christianity), [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Christianity) and [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Christianity).[^cite_note-122]

### Creeds

Main articles: [Christian creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_creed) and [List of Christian creeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_creeds)

Wikisource logo

English [Wikisource](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikisource) has original text related to this article:&#x20;

**[Apostles' Creed](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/en\:Apostles'%20Creed)**

Wikisource logo

English [Wikisource](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikisource) has original text related to this article:&#x20;

**[Nicene Creed](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/en\:Nicene%20Creed)**



An [Eastern Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity) icon depicting [Emperor Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great) and the Fathers of the [First Council of Nicaea](11118.md) (325) as holding the Niceno–Constantinopolitan Creed of 381

Concise doctrinal statements or confessions of religious beliefs are known as [creeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creeds). They began as baptismal formulae and were later expanded during the [Christological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology) controversies of the 4th and 5th centuries to become statements of faith. "[Jesus is Lord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_is_Lord)" is the earliest creed of Christianity and continues to be used, as with the [World Council of Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Council_of_Churches).[^cite_note-123]

The [Apostles' Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles'_Creed) is the most widely accepted statement of the articles of Christian faith. It is used by a number of [Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_denominations) for both [liturgical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy) and [catechetical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechesis) purposes, most visibly by liturgical churches of [Western Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christian) tradition, including the [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church) of the [Catholic Church](606848.md), [Lutheranism](23371382.md), [Anglicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism), and [Western Rite Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Rite_Orthodoxy). It is also used by [Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterianism), [Methodists](20119.md), and [Congregationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_church).

This particular creed was developed between the 2nd and 9th centuries. Its central doctrines are those of the [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity) and [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Christianity) the [Creator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creator_deity). Each of the doctrines found in this creed can be traced to statements current in the [apostolic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Age). The creed was apparently used as a summary of Christian doctrine for baptismal candidates in the churches of Rome.[^cite_note-124] Its points include:

- Belief in [God the Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Father), [Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_Christ) as the [Son of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Son),

  [^cite_note-125]

  [^cite_note-126]

  [^cite_note-127]

  [\[note 2\]](#cite_note-nontrinitarianism-9)

  &#x20;and the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_\(Christianity\))

- The [death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus), [descent into hell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrowing_of_Hell), [resurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus) and [ascension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus) of Christ

- The holiness of the [Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Church) and the [communion of saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communion_of_saints)

- Christ's [second coming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_coming), the [Day of Judgement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Judgment) and [salvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvation_in_Christianity) of the faithful

The [Nicene Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Creed) was formulated, largely in response to [Arianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arianism), at the Councils of [Nicaea](11118.md) and [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Council_of_Constantinople) in 325 and 381 respectively,[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] and ratified as the universal creed of [Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom) by the [First Council of Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Council_of_Ephesus) in 431.[^cite_note-130]

The [Chalcedonian Definition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcedonian_Definition), or Creed of Chalcedon, developed at the [Council of Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon) in 451,[^cite_note-131] though rejected by the [Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox),[^cite_note-132] taught Christ "to be acknowledged in two natures, inconfusedly, unchangeably, indivisibly, inseparably": one divine and one human, and that both natures, while perfect in themselves, are nevertheless also [perfectly united into one person](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypostatic_union).[^cite_note-133]

The [Athanasian Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athanasian_Creed), received in the Western Church as having the same status as the Nicene and Chalcedonian, says: "We worship one God in Trinity, and Trinity in Unity; neither confounding the Persons nor dividing the [Substance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consubstantiality)".[^cite_note-134]

Most Christians ([Catholic](606848.md), [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox), [Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox), and [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) alike) accept the use of creeds and subscribe to at least one of the creeds mentioned above.[^cite_note-UMC—Our_Common_Heritage_as_Christians-51]

Certain [Evangelical Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Protestants), though not all of them, reject creeds as definitive statements of faith, even while agreeing with some or all of the substance of the creeds. Also rejecting creeds are groups with roots in the [Restoration Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_Movement), such as the [Christian Church (Disciples of Christ)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Church_\(Disciples_of_Christ\)), the [Evangelical Christian Church in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Christian_Church_in_Canada), and the [Churches of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churches_of_Christ).[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136]<sup>: 14–15 </sup>[^cite_note-137]<sup>: 123 </sup>

### Jesus

Main articles: [Jesus in Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Christianity) and [Christ (title)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_\(title\))

See also: [Incarnation (Christianity)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incarnation_\(Christianity\)) and [Jesus in comparative mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_comparative_mythology)



Various [depictions of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depiction_of_Jesus)

The central tenet of Christianity is the belief in [Jesus](1095706.md) as the [Son of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_of_God_\(Christianity\))[^cite_note-nontrinitarianism-9] and the [Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah) (Christ).[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] Christians believe that Jesus, as the Messiah, was [anointed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anointing) by God as savior of humanity and hold that Jesus' coming was the fulfillment of [messianic prophecies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_messianic_prophecies) of the [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament). The Christian concept of messiah differs significantly from [the contemporary Jewish concept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism). The core Christian belief is that through belief in and acceptance of [the death and resurrection of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_Resurrection_of_Jesus), [sinful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_sin) humans can be reconciled to God, and thereby are offered salvation and the promise of [eternal life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immortality).[^cite_note-140]

While there have been many [theological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_theology) disputes over the [nature of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology) over the earliest centuries of Christian history, generally, Christians believe that Jesus is [God incarnate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incarnation_\(Christianity\)) and "[true God and true man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypostatic_union)" (or both fully divine and fully human). Jesus, having become fully human, suffered the pains and temptations of a mortal man, but did not [sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_views_on_sin). As fully God, he rose to life again. According to the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament), he [rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus) from the dead,[^cite_note-141] [ascended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus) to heaven, is seated at the right hand of the Father,[^cite_note-142] and will ultimately [return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming)[^cite_note-143] to fulfill the rest of the [Messianic prophecy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messianic_prophecy), including the [resurrection of the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_the_dead), the [Last Judgment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Judgment), and the final establishment of the [Kingdom of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_God_\(Christianity\)).

According to the [canonical gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_gospels) of [Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew) and [Luke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke), Jesus was [conceived](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incarnation_\(Christianity\)) by the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_\(Christianity\)) and [born](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativity_of_Jesus) from [the Virgin Mary](73513.md). Little of Jesus' childhood is recorded in the canonical gospels, although [infancy gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infancy_gospels) were popular in antiquity.[^cite_note-144] In comparison, his adulthood, especially the week before his death, is well documented in the gospels contained within the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament), because that part of his life is believed to be most important. The biblical accounts of [Jesus' ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Jesus) include: [his baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_Jesus), [miracles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miracles_of_Jesus), preaching, teaching, and deeds.

#### Death and resurrection

Main articles: [Crucifixion of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus) and [Resurrection of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus)



*[Crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_Crucified_\(Vel%C3%A1zquez\))*, representing the death of [Jesus](1095706.md) on the [Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Cross), painting by [Diego Velázquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez), c. 1632

Christians consider the resurrection of Jesus to be the cornerstone of their faith (see [1 Corinthians 15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1_Corinthians_15)).[^cite_note-145] Among Christian beliefs, the death and resurrection of Jesus are two core events on which much of Christian doctrine and theology is based.[^cite_note-146] According to the New Testament, Jesus was [crucified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion), died a physical death, was buried within a tomb, and rose from the dead three days later.[^cite_note-147]

The [New Testament mentions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overview_of_resurrection_appearances_in_the_Gospels_and_Paul) several [post-resurrection appearances of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus#Biblical_accounts) on different occasions to his [twelve apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_apostles) and [disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disciple_\(Christianity\)), including "more than five hundred brethren at once",[^cite_note-148] before Jesus' [ascension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus) to heaven. Jesus' death and resurrection are commemorated by Christians in all worship services, with special emphasis during [Holy Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Week), which includes [Good Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Friday) and [Easter](9325.md) Sunday.

The death and resurrection of Jesus are usually considered the most important events in [Christian theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_theology), partly because they demonstrate that Jesus has power over life and death and therefore has the authority and power to give people [eternal life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternal_life_\(Christianity\)).[^cite_note-149]

Christian churches accept and teach the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) account of the resurrection of Jesus with very few exceptions.[^cite_note-150] Some modern scholars use the belief of Jesus' followers in the resurrection as a point of departure for establishing the continuity of the [historical Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Jesus) and the proclamation of the [early church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_church).[^cite_note-151] Some [liberal Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Christianity) do not accept a literal bodily resurrection,[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] seeing the story as richly symbolic and spiritually nourishing [myth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mythology). Arguments over death and resurrection claims occur at many religious [debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debate) and [interfaith dialogues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_dialogue).[^cite_note-154] [Paul the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle), an early Christian convert and missionary, wrote, "If Christ was not raised, then all our preaching is useless, and your trust in God is useless".[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156]

### Salvation

Main article: [Salvation in Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvation_in_Christianity)

> "For God so loved the world that he gave his one and only Son, that whoever believes in him shall not perish but have eternal life".

— John 3\:16, NIV

[^cite_note-157]



*[The Law and the Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_and_Gospel_\(Cranach\))* by Lucas Cranach the Elder (1529); Moses and Elijah point the sinner to Jesus for salvation.

[Paul the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle), like Jews and Roman [pagans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagan) of his time, believed that sacrifice can bring about new kinship ties, purity, and eternal life.[^cite_note-remedy-158] For Paul, the necessary sacrifice was the death of Jesus: Gentiles who are "Christ's" are, like Israel, descendants of Abraham and "heirs according to the promise"[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160] The God who raised Jesus from the dead would also give new life to the "mortal bodies" of Gentile Christians, who had become with Israel, the "children of God", and were therefore no longer "in the flesh".[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-remedy-158]

Modern Christian churches tend to be much more concerned with how humanity can be [saved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternal_salvation) from a universal condition of sin and death than the question of how both Jews and Gentiles can be in God's family. According to [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) theology, based upon their understanding of the atonement as put forward by Irenaeus' [recapitulation theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recapitulation_\(Irenaeus\)), Jesus' death is a [ransom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ransom_theory_of_atonement). This restores the relation with God, who is loving and reaches out to humanity, and offers the possibility of *[theosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theosis_\(Eastern_Orthodox_theology\))* c.q. [divinization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divinization_\(Christian\)), becoming the kind of humans God wants humanity to be. The Catholic church also holds to a form of ransom theory[^cite_note-162] but also teaches that Jesus' death [satisfies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satisfaction_theory_of_atonement) the debt to the Father incurred by the sins of humanity.[^cite_note-163] The Catholic Church further affirms that salvation does not occur without faithfulness on the part of Christians; converts must live in accordance with principles of love and ordinarily must be baptized.[^cite_note-164] In Protestant theology, Jesus' death is regarded as a [substitutionary penalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penal_substitution) carried by Jesus, for the debt that has to be paid by humankind when it broke God's moral law.[^cite_note-165]

Christians differ in their views on the extent to which individuals' salvation is pre-ordained by God. Reformed theology places distinctive emphasis on grace by teaching that individuals are [completely incapable of self-redemption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_depravity), but that [sanctifying grace is irresistible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irresistible_grace).[^cite_note-166] In contrast [Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholics), Orthodox Christians, and [Arminian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arminianism) Protestants believe that the exercise of [free will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_will) is necessary to have faith in Jesus.[^cite_note-167]

### Trinity

Main article: [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity)



The [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity) is the belief that [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Christianity) is one God in three persons: the [Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Father), the [Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Son) ([Jesus](1095706.md)), and the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_\(Christianity\)).

[^cite_note-168]

*Trinity* refers to the teaching that the one God[^cite_note-169] comprises three distinct, eternally co-existing persons: the *[Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Father)*, the *Son* (incarnate in Jesus Christ) and the *[Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_in_Christianity)*. Together, these three persons are sometimes called the [Godhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Godhead_\(Christianity\)),[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] although there is no single term in use in Scripture to denote the unified Godhead.[^cite_note-173] In the words of the [Athanasian Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athanasian_Creed), an early statement of Christian belief, "the Father is God, the Son is God, and the Holy Spirit is God, and yet there are not three Gods but one God".[^cite_note-174] They are distinct from another: the Father has no source, the Son is begotten of the Father, and the Spirit proceeds from the Father. Though distinct, the three persons cannot be divided from one another in being or in operation. While some Christians also believe that God appeared as the Father in the [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament), it is agreed that he appeared as the Son in the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) and will still continue to manifest as the Holy Spirit in the present. But still, God still existed as three persons in each of these times.[^cite_note-175] However, traditionally there is a belief that it was the Son who appeared in the Old Testament because, for example, when the [Trinity is depicted in art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity#Art), the Son typically has the distinctive appearance, a [cruciform halo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cruciform_halo) identifying Christ, and in depictions of the [Garden of Eden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_of_Eden), this looks forward to an Incarnation yet to occur. In some [Early Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christian_art) [sarcophagi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarcophagi), the Logos is distinguished with a beard, "which allows him to appear ancient, even pre-existent".[^cite_note-176]

The [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity) is an essential doctrine of mainstream Christianity. From earlier than the times of the Nicene Creed (325) Christianity advocated[^cite_note-177] the triune [mystery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Mystery)-nature of [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Christianity) as a normative profession of faith. According to [Roger E. Olson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_E._Olson) and Christopher Hall, through prayer, meditation, study and practice, the Christian community concluded "that God must exist as both a unity and trinity", codifying this in ecumenical council at the end of the 4th century.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179]

According to this doctrine, God is not divided in the sense that each person has a third of the whole; rather, each person is considered to be fully God (see [Perichoresis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perichoresis)). The distinction lies in their relations, the Father being unbegotten; the Son being begotten of the Father; and the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father and (in [Western Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christian) theology) [from the Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filioque). Regardless of this apparent difference, the three "persons" are each [eternal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternity) and [omnipotent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omnipotent). Other Christian religions including [Unitarian Universalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarian_Universalism), [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses), and [Mormonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormonism), do not share those views on the Trinity.

The Greek word *trias*[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-Trias-Trinitas-184] is first seen in this sense in the works of [Theophilus of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophilus_of_Antioch); his text reads: "of the Trinity, of God, and of His Word, and of His Wisdom".[^cite_note-185] The term may have been in use before this time; its Latin equivalent,[^cite_note-Trias-Trinitas-184] *trinitas*,[^cite_note-Trinitas-182] appears afterwards with an explicit reference to the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, in [Tertullian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertullian).[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] In the following century, the word was in general use. It is found in many passages of [Origen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origen).[^cite_note-188]

#### Trinitarianism

Main article: [Trinitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinitarianism)

*Trinitarianism* denotes Christians who believe in the concept of the [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity). Almost all Christian denominations and churches hold Trinitarian beliefs. Although the words "Trinity" and "Triune" do not appear in the Bible, beginning in the 3rd century theologians developed the term and concept to facilitate [apprehension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apprehension_\(understanding\)) of the New Testament teachings of God as being Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. Since that time, Christian theologians have been careful to emphasize that Trinity does not imply that there are three gods (the antitrinitarian heresy of [Tritheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tritheism)), nor that each hypostasis of the Trinity is one-third of an infinite God (partialism), nor that the Son and the Holy Spirit are beings created by and subordinate to the Father ([Arianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arianism)). Rather, the Trinity is defined as one God in three persons.[^cite_note-189]

#### Nontrinitarianism

Main article: [Nontrinitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nontrinitarianism)

*Nontrinitarianism* (or *antitrinitarianism*) refers to theology that rejects the doctrine of the Trinity. Various nontrinitarian views, such as [adoptionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adoptionism) or [modalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modalism), existed in early Christianity, leading to disputes about [Christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology).[^cite_note-190] Nontrinitarianism reappeared in the [Gnosticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnosticism) of the [Cathars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathars) between the 11th and 13th centuries, among groups with [Unitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarianism) theology in the [Protestant Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Reformation) of the 16th century,[^cite_note-191] in the 18th-century [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment), among [Restorationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restorationism) groups arising during the [Second Great Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Great_Awakening) of the 19th century, and most recently, in [Oneness Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oneness_Pentecostalism) churches.

### Eschatology

Main article: [Christian eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_eschatology)

The end of things, whether the end of an individual life, the end of the age, or the end of the world, broadly speaking, is Christian eschatology; the study of the destiny of humans as it is revealed in the Bible. The major issues in Christian eschatology are the [Tribulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Tribulation), death and the afterlife, (mainly for [Evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Christianity) groups) [the Millennium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennialism) and the following [Rapture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapture), the [Second Coming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming) of Jesus, [Resurrection of the Dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_the_Dead), Heaven, (for [liturgical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_liturgy) branches) [Purgatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purgatory), and Hell, the [Last Judgment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Judgment), the end of the world, and the [New Heavens and New Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Heavens_and_New_Earth).

Christians believe that the second coming of Christ will occur at the [end of time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eschatology), after a period of severe persecution (the Great Tribulation). All who have died will be [resurrected bodily from the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_the_dead) for the Last Judgment. Jesus will fully establish the [Kingdom of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_God) in fulfillment of [scriptural prophecies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_prophecy).[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]

#### Death and afterlife

Most Christians believe that human beings experience divine judgment and are rewarded either with eternal life or [eternal damnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hell). This includes the [general judgement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Judgment) at the [resurrection of the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_the_dead) as well as the belief (held by Catholics,[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] Orthodox[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] and most Protestants) in a [judgment particular to the individual soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particular_judgment) after physical death.
