In the Catholic branch of Christianity, those who die in a state of grace, i.e., without any mortal sin separating them from God, but are still imperfectly purified from the effects of sin, undergo purification through the intermediate state of [purgatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purgatory) to achieve the holiness necessary for entrance into God's presence.[^cite_note-198] Those who have attained this goal are called *saints* (Latin *sanctus*, "holy").[^cite_note-199]

Some Christian groups, such as Seventh-day Adventists, hold to [mortalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_mortalism), the belief that the human soul is not naturally immortal, and is unconscious during the intermediate state between bodily death and resurrection. These Christians also hold to [Annihilationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annihilationism), the belief that subsequent to the final judgement, the wicked will cease to exist rather than suffer everlasting torment. Jehovah's Witnesses hold to a similar view.[^cite_note-200]

## Practices

Main articles: [Christian worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_worship) and [Church service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_service)

See also: [Mass (liturgy)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_\(liturgy\)), [Reformed worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_worship), and [Contemporary worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_worship)



Christmas Eve [Midnight Mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midnight_Mass) at a Catholic parish church in [Woodside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodside,_Queens), New York City, U.S.



[Old Order Anabaptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Order_Anabaptist) and [Conservative Anabaptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Anabaptist) women, for modesty, wear [cape dresses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_dress) and [head coverings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_head_covering), the latter of which is taught as a [church ordinance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinance_\(Christianity\)).

[^cite_note-Hartzler2013-201]

Depending on the specific [denomination of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination), practices may include [baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism), the [Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist) (Holy Communion or the Lord's Supper), [prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_prayer) (including the [Lord's Prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord's_Prayer)), [confession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confession_\(religion\)), [confirmation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation), [burial rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_burial), [marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_views_on_marriage) rites and the religious education of children. Most denominations have [ordained](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordination#Christianity) [clergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clergy#Christianity) who lead regular [communal](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/communal) worship services.[^cite_note-White_71-82-202]

[Christian rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rite_\(Christianity\)), rituals, and ceremonies are not celebrated in one single [sacred language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_language). Many ritualistic Christian churches make a distinction between sacred language, liturgical language and vernacular language. The three important languages in the [early Christian era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity) were: [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) and [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language).[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205]

### Communal worship

[Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_service) of worship typically follow a pattern or form known as [liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_liturgy).[^cite_note-207] [Justin Martyr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Martyr) described 2nd-century [Christian liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_liturgy) in his *[First Apology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Apology)* (c. 150) to Emperor [Antoninus Pius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoninus_Pius), and his description remains relevant to the basic structure of Christian liturgical worship:

> And Sundays, all who live in cities or in the country gather together to one place, and the memoirs of the apostles or the writings of the prophets are read, as long as time permits; then, when the reader has ceased, the [president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyter) verbally instructs, and exhorts to the imitation of these good things. Then we all rise together and pray, and, as we before said, when our prayer is ended, bread and wine and water are brought, and the president in like manner offers prayers and thanksgivings, according to his ability, and the people assent, saying [Amen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amen); and there is a distribution to each, and a participation of that over which thanks have been given, and to those who are absent a portion is sent by the deacons. And they who are well to do, and willing, give what each thinks fit; and what is collected is deposited with the president, who succours the orphans and widows and those who, through sickness or any other cause, are in want, and those who are in bonds and the strangers sojourning among us, and in a word takes care of all who are in need.[^cite_note-justin-208]

Thus, as Justin described, Christians assemble for communal worship typically on Sunday, the [Lord's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord's_Day), though other liturgical practices often occur outside this setting. Scripture readings are drawn from the Old and New Testaments, but especially the gospels.[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210] Instruction is given based on these readings, in the form of a [sermon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon) or [homily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homily). There are a variety of [congregational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_\(congregation\)) prayers, including thanksgiving, confession, and [intercession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercession), which occur throughout the service and take a variety of forms including recited, responsive, silent, or sung.[^cite_note-White_71-82-202] [Psalms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psalms), [hymns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hymn#Christian_hymnody), [worship songs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_worship_music), and other [church music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_music) may be sung.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212] Services can be varied for special events like significant [feast days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendar_of_saints).[^cite_note-213]



[*The Last Supper*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Last_Supper_\(Leonardo\)) in the [Santa Maria delle Grazie Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Maria_delle_Grazie,_Milan) in [Milan](36511.md), [Italy](14532.md), a late 1490s mural painting by [Leonardo da Vinci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci), depicting the [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper) of Jesus and his [twelve apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament), depicts the [final meal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper) of [Jesus](1095706.md) before [his crucifixion and death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus).

Nearly all forms of worship incorporate the Eucharist, which consists of bread and wine (or a substitute). It is reenacted in accordance with Jesus' instruction at the [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper) that his followers do in remembrance of him as when he gave his disciples [bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramental_bread), saying, "This is my body", and gave them [wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramental_wine) saying, "This is my blood".[^cite_note-214] In the [early church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity), Christians and those yet to complete initiation would separate for the Eucharistic part of the service.[^cite_note-215] Some denominations such as [Confessional Lutheran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confessional_Lutheranism) churches continue to practice '[closed communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closed_communion)'.[^cite_note-216] They offer communion to those who are already united in that denomination or sometimes individual church. Catholics further restrict participation to their members who are not in a state of [mortal sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortal_sin).[^cite_note-217] Many other churches, such as the [Anglican Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Communion) and the [Methodist Churches](20119.md) (such as the [Free Methodist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Methodist_Church) and [United Methodist Church](32304.md)), practice '[open communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_communion)' since they view communion as a means to unity, rather than an end, and invite all believing Christians to participate.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220]

### Sacraments or ordinances

Main article: [Sacrament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrament)

See also: [Sacraments of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacraments_of_the_Catholic_Church), [Lutheran sacraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutheran_sacraments), [Anglican sacraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_sacraments), and [Ordinance (Christianity)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinance_\(Christianity\))

Further information: [Rite (Christianity)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rite_\(Christianity\)) and [Pre-Tridentine Mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Tridentine_Mass)

2nd-century description of the [Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist)

> And this food is called among us *Eukharistia* \[the Eucharist], of which no one is allowed to partake but the man who believes that the things which we teach are true, and who has been washed with the washing that is for the remission of sins, and unto regeneration, and who is so living as Christ has enjoined. For not as common bread and common drink do we receive these; but in like manner as Jesus Christ our Savior, having been made flesh by the Word of God, had both flesh and blood for our salvation, so likewise have we been taught that the food which is blessed by the prayer of His word, and from which our blood and flesh by transmutation are nourished, is the flesh and blood of that Jesus who was made flesh.

— [Justin Martyr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Martyr)

[^cite_note-justin-208]

In Christian belief and practice, a *sacrament* is a [rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ritual), instituted by Christ, that confers [grace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_grace), constituting a [sacred mystery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_Mysteries). The term is derived from the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) word *sacramentum*, which was used to translate the Greek word for *mystery*. Views concerning both which rites are sacramental, and what it means for an act to be a sacrament, vary among Christian denominations and traditions.[^cite_note-ODCC1435-221]

The most conventional functional definition of a sacrament is that it is an outward sign, instituted by Christ, that conveys an inward, spiritual grace through Christ. The two most widely accepted sacraments are [Baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism) and the Eucharist; however, the majority of Christians also recognize five additional sacraments: [Confirmation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_\(Christian_sacrament\)) ([Chrismation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrismation) in the Eastern tradition), [Holy Orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Orders) (or [ordination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordination)), [Penance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penance) (or [Confession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confession_\(religion\))), [Anointing of the Sick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anointing_of_the_Sick), and [Matrimony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matrimony) (see [Christian views on marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_views_on_marriage)).[^cite_note-ODCC1435-221]

Taken together, these are the [Seven Sacraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacraments_of_the_Catholic_Church) as recognized by churches in the [High Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Church) tradition—notably Catholic, [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox), [Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox), [Independent Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Catholic_Churches), [Old Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Catholic), some [Lutherans](23371382.md) and [Anglicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_sacraments). Most other denominations and traditions typically affirm only Baptism and Eucharist as sacraments, while some Protestant groups, such as the Quakers, reject sacramental theology.[^cite_note-ODCC1435-221] Certain denominations of Christianity, such as Anabaptists, use the term "[ordinances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinance_\(Christianity\))" to refer to rites instituted by Jesus for Christians to observe.[^cite_note-222] Seven ordinances have been taught in many [Conservative Mennonite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Mennonite) Anabaptist churches, which include "baptism, communion, footwashing, marriage, anointing with oil, the holy kiss, and the prayer covering".[^cite_note-Hartzler2013-201]

In addition to this, the [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East) has two additional sacraments in place of the traditional sacraments of Matrimony and the Anointing of the Sick. These include [Holy Leaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Leaven) (Melka) and the [sign of the cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sign_of_the_cross).[^cite_note-223] The [Schwarzenau Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwarzenau_Brethren) Anabaptist churches, such as the [Dunkard Brethren Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunkard_Brethren_Church), observe the [agape feast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agape_feast) (lovefeast), a [rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rite_\(Christianity\)) also observed by [Moravian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moravian_Church) and [Methodist Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist_Church).[^cite_note-224]

### Liturgical calendar

Main article: [Liturgical year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical_year)

See also: [Calendar of saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendar_of_saints)

Catholics, Eastern Christians, Lutherans, Anglicans and other traditional Protestant communities frame worship around the [liturgical year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical_year).[^cite_note-225] The liturgical cycle divides the year into a series of [seasons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Season), each with their theological emphases, and modes of prayer, which can be signified by different ways of decorating churches, colors of [paraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parament) and [vestments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vestment) for clergy,[^cite_note-calendar-226] scriptural readings, themes for preaching and even different traditions and practices often observed personally or in the home.

Western Christian liturgical calendars are based on the cycle of the [Roman Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Rite) of the Catholic Church,[^cite_note-calendar-226] and Eastern Christians use analogous calendars based on the cycle of their respective [rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_rite). Calendars set aside holy days, such as [solemnities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solemnity) which commemorate an event in the life of Jesus, Mary, or the [saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint), and periods of [fasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasting), such as [Lent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lent) and other pious events such as [memoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memoria), or lesser festivals commemorating saints. Christian groups that do not follow a liturgical tradition often retain certain celebrations, such as [Christmas](6237.md), [Easter](9325.md), and [Pentecost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecost): these are the celebrations of Christ's birth, resurrection, and the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the Church, respectively. A few denominations such as [Quaker Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers) make no use of a liturgical calendar.[^cite_note-227]

### Symbols

Main article: [Christian symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_symbolism)



An early circular [ichthys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ichthys) symbol, created by combining the Greek letters [ΙΧΘΥΣ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CE%99%CE%A7%CE%98%CE%A5%CE%A3) into a wheel, [Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ephesus), Asia Minor

Most Christian denominations have not generally practiced [aniconism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aniconism_in_Christianity),[^cite_note-228] the avoidance or prohibition of devotional images, even if early [Jewish Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Christian), invoking the [Decalogue's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_Commandments) prohibition of [idolatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatry), avoided figures in their symbols.[^cite_note-229]

The [cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_cross), today one of the most widely recognized symbols, was used by Christians from the earliest times.[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] Tertullian, in his book *De Corona*, tells how it was already a tradition for Christians to trace the sign of the cross on their foreheads.[^cite_note-232] Although the cross was known to the early Christians, the [crucifix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifix) did not appear in use until the 5th century.[^cite_note-soc-233]

Among the earliest Christian symbols, that of the fish or [Ichthys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ichthys) seems to have ranked first in importance, as seen on monumental sources such as tombs from the first decades of the 2nd century.[^cite_note-cefish-234] Its popularity seemingly arose from the Greek word *ichthys* (fish) forming an [acrostic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acrostic) for the Greek phrase *Iesous Christos Theou Yios Soter* (Ἰησοῦς Χριστός, Θεοῦ Υἱός, Σωτήρ),[^cite_note-235] (Jesus Christ, Son of God, Savior), a concise summary of Christian faith.[^cite_note-cefish-234]

Other major Christian symbols include the [chi-rho monogram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chi-Rho), the [dove and olive branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dove_of_peace) (symbolic of the Holy Spirit), the sacrificial lamb (representing Christ's sacrifice), the [vine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vine) (symbolizing the connection of the Christian with Christ) and many others. These all derive from passages of the New Testament.[^cite_note-soc-233]

### Baptism

Main article: [Baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism)



[Infant baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_baptism) by effusion in a [Catholic Church in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Venezuela)



[Believer's baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Believer's_baptism) of adult by immersion, [Northolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northolt) Park Baptist Church, in [Greater London](45367389.md), [Baptist Union of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptist_Union_of_Great_Britain)

Baptism is the ritual act, with the use of water, by which a person is admitted to membership of the [Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Church). Beliefs on baptism vary among denominations. Differences occur firstly on whether the act has any spiritual significance. Some, such as the Catholic and [Eastern Orthodox churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_churches), as well as Lutherans and Anglicans, hold to the doctrine of [baptismal regeneration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptismal_regeneration), which affirms that baptism creates or strengthens a person's faith, and is intimately linked to salvation. [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists) and [Plymouth Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plymouth_Brethren) view baptism as a purely symbolic act, an external public declaration of the inward change which has taken place in the person, but not as spiritually efficacious. Secondly, there are differences of opinion on the methodology (or mode) of the act. These modes are: by [*immersion*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immersion_baptism); if immersion is total, by *submersion*; by [affusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affusion) (pouring); and by [aspersion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspersion) (sprinkling). Those who hold the first view may also adhere to the tradition of [infant baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_baptism);[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239] the Orthodox Churches all practice infant baptism and always baptize by total immersion repeated three times in the name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] The Lutheran Church and the Catholic Church also practice infant baptism,[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] usually by affusion, and using the [Trinitarian formula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinitarian_formula).[^cite_note-245] [Anabaptist Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anabaptist_Christian) practice [believer's baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Believer's_baptism), in which an adult chooses to receive the ordinance after making a decision to follow Jesus.[^cite_note-246] Anabaptist denominations such as the [Mennonites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mennonites), [Amish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amish) and [Hutterites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutterites) use pouring as the mode to administer believer's baptism, whereas Anabaptists of the [Schwarzenau Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwarzenau_Brethren) and [River Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Brethren) traditions baptize by immersion.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250]

### Prayer

Main article: [Christian prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_prayer)

See also: [Christian meditation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_meditation) and [Canonical hours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_hours)

> "... 'Our Father in heaven, hallowed be your name. Your kingdom come. Your will be done on earth as it is in heaven. Give us today our daily bread. Forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors. Lead us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil'".

— The [Lord's Prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord's_Prayer), Matthew 6\:9–13, [EHV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Heritage_Version)

[^cite_note-251]

In the [Gospel of Saint Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Saint_Matthew), Jesus taught the [Lord's Prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord's_Prayer), which has been seen as a model for Christian prayer.[^cite_note-252] The injunction for Christians to pray the Lord's prayer thrice daily was given in the *[Didache](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didache)* and came to be recited by Christians at 9 am, 12 pm, and 3 pm.[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254]

In the second century *[Apostolic Tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Tradition)*, [Hippolytus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippolytus_of_Rome) instructed Christians to pray at [seven fixed prayer times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed_prayer_times): "on rising, at the lighting of the evening lamp, at bedtime, at midnight" and "the third, sixth and ninth hours of the day, being hours associated with Christ's Passion".[^cite_note-255] Prayer positions, including kneeling, standing, and [prostrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostration) have been used for these seven fixed prayer times since the days of the early Church.[^cite_note-256] [Breviaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breviary) such as the [Shehimo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shehimo) and [Agpeya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agpeya) are used by [Oriental Orthodox Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Christians) to pray these [canonical hours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_hours) while facing in the [eastward direction of prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direction_of_prayer).[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258]

The *Apostolic Tradition* directed that the [sign of the cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sign_of_the_cross) be used by Christians during the [minor exorcism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_exorcism_in_Christianity) of [baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism), during [ablutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ablution_in_Christianity) before praying at fixed prayer times, and in times of temptation.[^cite_note-259]

*Intercessory prayer* is prayer offered for the benefit of other people. There are many intercessory prayers recorded in the Bible, including prayers of the [Apostle Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostle_Peter) on behalf of sick persons[^cite_note-260] and by [prophets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophet) of the Old Testament in favor of other people.[^cite_note-261] In the [Epistle of James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistle_of_James), no distinction is made between the intercessory prayer offered by ordinary believers and the prominent Old Testament prophet [Elijah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elijah).[^cite_note-262] The effectiveness of prayer in Christianity derives from the power of God rather than the status of the one praying.[^cite_note-263]

The ancient church, in both [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity) and [Western Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity), developed a tradition of asking for the [intercession of (deceased) saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercession_of_saints), and this remains the practice of most [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox), [Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox), [Catholic](606848.md), and some [Lutheran](23371382.md) and [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican) churches.[^cite_note-264] Apart from certain sectors within the latter two denominations, other Churches of the Protestant Reformation, however, rejected prayer to the saints, largely on the basis of the sole mediatorship of Christ.[^cite_note-265] The reformer [Huldrych Zwingli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huldrych_Zwingli) admitted that he had offered prayers to the saints until his reading of the Bible convinced him that this was [idolatrous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatry_in_Christianity).[^cite_note-266]

According to the *[Catechism of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church)*: "Prayer is the raising of one's mind and heart to God or the requesting of good things from God".[^cite_note-267] The *[Book of Common Prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Common_Prayer)* in the Anglican tradition is a guide which provides a set order for services, containing set prayers, scripture readings, and hymns or sung Psalms.[^cite_note-268] Frequently in Western Christianity, when praying, the hands are placed palms together and forward as in the feudal [commendation ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commendation_ceremony). At other times the older [orans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orans) posture may be used, with palms up and elbows in.

### Marriage

Main article: [Christian views on marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_views_on_marriage)

Most Christian churches in the world only support [marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage) between a man and a woman.[^cite_note-269]

Some Christian churches allow local churches to decide about [blessings of same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blessing_of_same-sex_unions_in_Christian_churches).[^cite_note-270]

Some international networks of [Affirming churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirming_church) support [blessings of same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blessing_of_same-sex_unions_in_Christian_churches), such the [Association of Welcoming and Affirming Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Welcoming_and_Affirming_Baptists),[^cite_note-AWAB-271] [Ecumenical Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Catholic_Church),[^cite_note-ECC-272] [Metropolitan Community Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Community_Church),[^cite_note-MCC2-273] Reconciling Ministries Network ([Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist)),[^cite_note-RMN-274] The Covenant Network ([Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostal)).[^cite_note-CN2-275]

## Scriptures

Main articles: [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible), [Biblical canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_canon), [Development of the Christian biblical canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_of_the_Christian_biblical_canon), and [Religious text](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_text)



The [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) is the sacred book in Christianity.

Christianity, like other religions, has adherents whose beliefs and biblical interpretations vary. Christianity regards the [biblical canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_canon), the [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament) and the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament), as the [inspired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_inspiration) word of God. The traditional view of inspiration is that God worked through human authors so that what they produced was what God wished to communicate. The Greek word referring to inspiration in [2 Timothy 3\:16](https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=2%20Timothy%203\:16\&version=nrsv) is *theopneustos*, which literally means "God-breathed".[^cite_note-276]

Some believe that divine inspiration makes present Bibles [inerrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_inerrancy), while others claim inerrancy for the Bible in its original manuscripts, although none of those are extant.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] Another closely related view is [biblical infallibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_infallibility) or limited inerrancy, which affirms that the Bible is free of error as a guide to salvation, but may include errors on matters such as history, geography, or science.

The canon of the Old Testament accepted by Protestant churches, which is only the [Tanakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanakh) (the canon of the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible)), is shorter than that accepted by the Orthodox and Catholic churches which also include the [deuterocanonical books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deuterocanonical_books) which appear in the [Septuagint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septuagint), the Orthodox canon being slightly larger than the Catholic;[^cite_note-280] Protestants regard the latter as [apocryphal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_apocrypha), important historical documents which help to inform the understanding of words, grammar, and syntax used in the historical period of their conception. Some versions of the Bible include a separate Apocrypha section between the Old Testament and the New Testament.[^cite_note-281] The New Testament, originally written in [Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek), contains 27 books which are agreed upon by all major churches.

Some denominations have [additional canonical holy scriptures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_religious_texts#Additional_and_alternative_scriptures) beyond the Bible, including the [standard works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_works) of the [Latter Day Saints movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latter_Day_Saints_movement) and *[Divine Principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Principle)* in the [Unification Church](512933.md).[^cite_note-282]

### Catholic interpretation

Main article: [Catholic theology of Scripture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_theology_of_Scripture)



[St. Peter's Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Peter's_Basilica) viewed from the [Tiber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiber); the [Vatican Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vatican_Hill) in the back and [Castel Sant'Angelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castel_Sant'Angelo) in [Rome](25458.md) to the right. Both the basilica and the hill are part of the [sovereign state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_observers) of [Vatican City](32408.md), the [Holy See](13393.md) of the [Catholic Church](606848.md).

In antiquity, two schools of exegesis developed in [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Alexandria) and [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Antioch). The Alexandrian interpretation, exemplified by [Origen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origen), tended to read Scripture [allegorically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegory), while the Antiochene interpretation adhered to the literal sense, holding that other meanings (called *[theoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theoria)*) could only be accepted if based on the literal meaning.[^cite_note-283]

[Catholic theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_theology) distinguishes two senses of scripture: the literal and the spiritual.[^cite_note-284]

The *literal* sense of understanding scripture is the meaning conveyed by the words of Scripture. The *spiritual* sense is further subdivided into:

- The *allegorical* sense, which includes [typology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typology_\(theology\)). An example would be the [parting of the Red Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passage_of_the_Red_Sea) being understood as a "type" (sign) of baptism.

  [^cite_note-285]

- The *[moral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral)* sense, which understands the scripture to contain some ethical teaching.

- The *[anagogical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anagoge)* sense, which applies to [eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eschatology), eternity and the [consummation of the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apocalypse).

Regarding [exegesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exegesis), following the rules of sound interpretation, Catholic theology holds:

- The injunction that all other senses of sacred scripture are based on the *literal*

  [^cite_note-286]

  [^cite_note-\:3-287]

- That the historicity of the Gospels must be absolutely and constantly held

  [^cite_note-288]

- That scripture must be read within the "living Tradition of the whole Church"

  [^cite_note-\:3-287]

  &#x20;and

- That "the task of interpretation has been entrusted to the bishops in communion with the successor of Peter, the [Bishop](4092.md) of [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese_of_Rome)".

  [^cite_note-289]

### Protestant interpretation

#### Qualities of Scripture

Many Protestant Christians, such as Lutherans[^cite_note-solascriptura-290] and the Reformed, believe in the doctrine of *[sola scriptura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sola_scriptura)*—that the Bible is a self-sufficient revelation, the final authority on all Christian doctrine, and [revealed all truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revelation) necessary for salvation;[^cite_note-WELS2014-291][^cite_note-292] other Protestant Christians, such as Methodists and Anglicans, affirm the doctrine of *[prima scriptura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prima_scriptura)* which teaches that Scripture is the primary source for Christian doctrine, but that "tradition, experience, and reason" can nurture the Christian religion as long as they are in harmony with the Bible.[^cite_note-WELS2014-291][^cite_note-293] Protestants characteristically believe that ordinary believers may reach an adequate understanding of Scripture because Scripture itself is clear in its meaning (or "perspicuous"). Martin Luther believed that without God's help, Scripture would be "enveloped in darkness".[^cite_note-Foutz-294] He advocated for "one definite and simple understanding of Scripture".[^cite_note-Foutz-294] [John Calvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Calvin) wrote, "all who refuse not to follow the Holy Spirit as their guide, find in the Scripture a clear light".[^cite_note-295] Related to this is "efficacy", that Scripture is able to lead people to faith; and "sufficiency", that the Scriptures contain everything that one needs to know to obtain salvation and to live a Christian life.[^cite_note-296]

#### Original intended meaning of Scripture

Protestants stress the meaning conveyed by the words of Scripture, the [historical-grammatical method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical-grammatical_method).[^cite_note-297] The historical-grammatical method or grammatico-historical method is an effort in [Biblical hermeneutics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_hermeneutics) to find the intended original meaning in the text.[^cite_note-Elwell565-298] This original intended meaning of the text is drawn out through examination of the passage in light of the grammatical and syntactical aspects, the historical background, the literary genre, as well as theological (canonical) considerations.[^cite_note-299] The historical-grammatical method distinguishes between the one original meaning and the significance of the text. The significance of the text includes the ensuing use of the text or application. The original passage is seen as having only a single meaning or sense. As [Milton S. Terry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milton_S._Terry) said: "A fundamental principle in grammatico-historical exposition is that the words and sentences can have but one significance in one and the same connection. The moment we neglect this principle we drift out upon a sea of uncertainty and conjecture".[^cite_note-300] Technically speaking, the grammatical-historical method of interpretation is distinct from the determination of the passage's significance in light of that interpretation. Taken together, both define the term (Biblical) hermeneutics.[^cite_note-Elwell565-298] Some Protestant interpreters make use of [typology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typology_\(theology\)).[^cite_note-301]

## Demographics

Main articles: [Christianity by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_by_country) and [Christian population growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_population_growth)

See also: [Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom) and [Christian state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_state)



Global percentage of adherents by religion. Christians are the world's largest religious group, with about 2.3 billion adherents in 2020, with about 28.8% of the world's population.

[^cite_note-Pew_20250609-302]

With around 2.3 billion adherents according to a 2020 estimation by [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center),[^cite_note-pew2010-2020-303][^cite_note-304] split into three main branches of Catholic, Protestant, and Eastern Orthodox, Christianity is the [world's largest religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_religious_groups).[^cite_note-pew2010-2020-303][^cite_note-PewDec2012-305] High birth rates and [conversions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Christianity) in the [global South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_South) were cited as the reasons for the Christian population growth.[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307] For the last hundred years, the Christian share has stood at around 33% of the world population. This masks a major shift in the demographics of Christianity; large increases in the developing world have been accompanied by substantial declines in the developed world, mainly in Western Europe and North America.[^cite_note-308] Based on the 2020 population estimate, Christians comprise 28.8% of the [world's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World's_population).[^cite_note-pew-309] 46.8% of the Christian population is concentrated in 10 countries: [United States](3434750.md) (over 210 million), [Brazil](3383.md) (over 160 million), [Mexico](3966054.md) (over 110 million), [Philippines](23440.md) and [Russia](25391.md) (each one over 100 million), [Nigeria](21383.md) and [Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DRC) (each one over 90 million), [Ethiopia](187749.md) (over 70 million), [South Africa](17416221.md) (over 50 million) and [Italy](14532.md) (over 40 million).[^cite_note-pew-309]



A Christian procession in [Brazil](3383.md), the country with the largest Catholic population in the world

[^cite_note-PewDec2012-305]



[Trinity Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Sunday) in [Russia](25391.md); the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) has experienced a great revival since the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), a country that had a policy of [state atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_atheism).

[^cite_note-Pew20152016-310]



Show on the life of [Jesus](1095706.md) at [City Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Church_\(Brazil\)) in [São José dos Campos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Jos%C3%A9_dos_Campos), affiliated with the [Brazilian Baptist Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Baptist_Convention)

Based on a 2015 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) study, within the next four decades, Christianity will remain the largest religion; and by 2050, the Christian population is expected to exceed 3 billion.[^cite_note-PewProjections-311]<sup>: 60 </sup> According to some scholars, Christianity ranks at first place in net gains through [religious conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conversion).[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313] As a percentage of Christians, the [Catholic Church](606848.md) and [Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodoxy) (both [Eastern](10186.md) and [Oriental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy)) are declining in some parts of the world (though Catholicism is growing in Asia, in Africa, vibrant in Eastern Europe, etc.), while [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestants) and other Christians are on the rise in the developing world.[^cite_note-pewforum1-314][^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316][^cite_note-gordonconwell.edu-317][^cite_note-KimOsmerSchweitzer2018-318][^cite_note-319] The so-called *popular Protestantism*[^cite_note-320] is one of the fastest growing religious categories in the world.[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] Nevertheless, Catholicism will also continue to grow to 1.63 billion by 2050, according to Todd Johnson of the Center for the Study of Global Christianity.[^cite_note-324] Africa alone, by 2015, will be home to 230 million African Catholics.[^cite_note-325] According to Pew Research Center, Africa is expected to be home to 1.1 billion [African Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Africa) by 2050.[^cite_note-PewProjections-311]

In 2010, 87% of the world's Christian population lived in countries where Christians are in the majority, while 13% of the world's Christian population lived in countries where Christians are in the minority.[^cite_note-Global_Christianity-1] Christianity is the predominant religion in Europe, the Americas, Oceania, and Sub-Saharan Africa.[^cite_note-Global_Christianity-1] There are also large Christian communities in other parts of the world, such as [Central Asia](6742.md), the [Middle East and North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_and_North_Africa), [East Asia](19605700.md), [Southeast Asia](28741.md), and the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent).[^cite_note-Global_Christianity-1] In Asia, it is the dominant religion in Armenia, Cyprus, Georgia, East Timor, and the Philippines.[^cite_note-326] However, it is declining in some areas including the northern and western United States,[^cite_note-327] some areas in Oceania (Australia[^cite_note-328] and New Zealand[^cite_note-CensusNZ_2023-329]), northern Europe (including Great Britain,[^cite_note-330] Scandinavia and other places), France, Germany, Canada,[^cite_note-331] and some parts of Asia (especially the Middle East, due to the [Christian emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_emigration),[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334] and Macau[^cite_note-335]).

The total Christian population is not decreasing in [Brazil](3383.md) and the [southern United States](179553.md),[^cite_note-336] however, the percentage of the population identifying as Christian is in decline. Since the fall of communism, the proportion of Christians has been largely stable in [Central Europe](5188.md), except in the [Czech Republic](5321.md).[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Center-337] On the other hand, Christianity is growing rapidly in both numbers and percentages in Eastern Europe,[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Center-337][^cite_note-Pew20152016-310] China,[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-PewDec2012-305] other [Asian countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_countries),[^cite_note-PewDec2012-305][^cite_note-Singapore_Management_University-339] [Sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md),[^cite_note-PewDec2012-305][^cite_note-The_Next_Christendom_2002-340] [Latin America](18524.md),[^cite_note-PewDec2012-305] [North Africa](21714.md) ([Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb)),[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-The_Next_Christendom_2002-340] [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md) countries,[^cite_note-PewDec2012-305] [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world),[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-342] and [Oceania](22621.md).[^cite_note-The_Next_Christendom_2002-340]

Despite a decline in adherence in the [West](21208200.md), Christianity remains the dominant religion in the region, with about 70% of that population identifying as Christian.[^cite_note-Global_Christianity-1][^cite_note-343] Christianity remains the largest religion in [Western Europe](33800.md), where 71% of Western Europeans identified themselves as Christian in 2018.[^cite_note-344] A 2011 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) survey found that 76% of Europeans, 73% in Oceania and about 86% in the Americas (90% in Latin America and 77% in North America) identified themselves as Christians.[^cite_note-PewDec2012-305][^cite_note-Global_Christianity-1] By 2020, 120 countries and territories in the world had [Christian majorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_by_country).[^cite_note-pew2010-2020-303]

There are many [charismatic movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charismatic_movements) that have become well established over large parts of the world, especially Africa, Latin America, and Asia.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349][^cite_note-Global_Christianity-1] Since 1900, primarily due to conversion, Protestantism has spread rapidly in Africa, Asia, Oceania, and Latin America.[^cite_note-350] From 1960 to 2000, the global growth of the number of reported [Evangelical Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Protestants) grew three times the world's population rate, and twice that of [Islam](6037917.md).[^cite_note-351] According to the historian [Geoffrey Blainey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Blainey) from the [University of Melbourne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Melbourne), since the 1960s there has been a substantial increase in the number of conversions from Islam to Christianity, mostly to the [Evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical) and [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostal) forms.[^cite_note-352][^cite_note-353] Significant numbers of Muslim converts to Christianity can be found in Afghanistan,[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-Miller-2014-354] Azerbaijan,[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-Miller-2014-354] Central Asia (including Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, and other countries),[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-Miller-2014-354] Indonesia,[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-Miller-2014-354] Malaysia,[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-Miller-2014-354] the Middle East (including Iran, Saudi Arabia, Turkey,[^cite_note-355] and other countries),[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-Miller-2014-354] North Africa (including Algeria, Morocco,[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357] and Tunisia[^cite_note-358]),[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-Miller-2014-354] Sub-Saharan Africa,[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-Miller-2014-354] and the Western World (including Albania, Belgium, France, Germany, Kosovo, the Netherlands, Russia, Scandinavia, United Kingdom, the United States, and other western countries).[^cite_note-Johnstone-2015-341][^cite_note-Miller-2014-354] It is also reported that Christianity is popular among people of different backgrounds in Africa and Asia; according to a report by the [Singapore Management University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Management_University), more people in Southeast Asia are converting to Christianity, many of them young and having a [university degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_degree).[^cite_note-Singapore_Management_University-339] According to scholar Juliette Koning and Heidi Dahles of [Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vrije_Universiteit_Amsterdam) there is a "rapid expansion" of [Christianity in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Singapore), China, Hong Kong, Taiwan, [Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Indonesia), [Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Malaysia), and South Korea.[^cite_note-Singapore_Management_University-339] According to scholar Terence Chong from the Institute of Southeast Asian Studies, since the 1980s Christianity is expanding in China, Singapore,[^cite_note-359] Indonesia, [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Japan),[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361] Malaysia, Taiwan, South Korea,[^cite_note-Global_Christianity-1] and Vietnam.[^cite_note-362]

In most countries in the developed world, [church attendance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_attendance) among people who continue to identify themselves as Christians has been falling over the last few decades.[^cite_note-363] Some sources view this as part of a drift away from traditional membership institutions,[^cite_note-364] while others link it to signs of a decline in belief in the importance of religion in general.[^cite_note-365] Europe's Christian population, though in decline, still constitutes the largest geographical component of the religion.[^cite_note-366] According to data from the 2012 European Social Survey, around a third of [European Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Europe) say they attend services once a month or more.[^cite_note-economist-367] Conversely, according to the [World Values Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Values_Survey), about more than two-thirds of Latin American Christians, and about 90% of [African Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Africa) (in Ghana, Nigeria, Rwanda, South Africa and Zimbabwe) said they attended church regularly.[^cite_note-economist-367] According to a 2018 study by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), Christians in Africa and Latin America and the United States have high levels of commitment to their faith.[^cite_note-368]

[Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_state), in one form or another, is the sole [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion) of the following nations: [Argentina](18951905.md) (Catholic),[^cite_note-369] [Costa Rica](5551.md) (Catholic),[^cite_note-370] the [Kingdom of Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Denmark) (Lutheran),[^cite_note-371] [England](9316.md) (Anglican),[^cite_note-372] [Greece](12108.md) (Greek Orthodox),[^cite_note-373] [Iceland](14531.md) (Lutheran),[^cite_note-374] Liechtenstein (Catholic),[^cite_note-375] [Malta](19137.md) (Catholic),[^cite_note-376] Monaco (Catholic),[^cite_note-377] [Norway](21241.md) (Lutheran),[^cite_note-378] [Samoa](27238.md),[^cite_note-379] [Tonga](30158.md) (Methodist), [Tuvalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuvalu) (Reformed), and [Vatican City](32408.md) (Catholic).[^cite_note-380]

There are numerous other countries, such as Cyprus, which although do not have an [established church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Established_church), still give official recognition and support to a specific [Christian denomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination).[^cite_note-381]

## Churches and denominations

Further information: [List of Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_denominations), [List of Christian denominations by number of members](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_denominations_by_number_of_members), and [Schism in Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schism_in_Christianity)

See also: [Ecclesiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiology)

World Christianity by tradition in 2024 as per World Christian Database

[^cite_note-WorldChristian_Database-383]

1. [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_by_country) (48.6%)

2. [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_by_country) (39.8%)

3. Orthodox (11.1%)

4. Other (0.50%)

Christianity can be taxonomically divided into three main groups: [Catholicism](606848.md), [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism), [Eastern Orthodoxy](10186.md) while other groups include [Oriental Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches), [Restorationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restorationism) and [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East).[^cite_note-384][^cite_note-385] A broader distinction that is sometimes drawn is between [Eastern Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity) and [Western Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity), which has its origins in the [East–West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) (Great Schism) of the 11th century. Recently, neither Western nor Eastern [World Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Christianity) has also stood out, for example, in [African-initiated churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-initiated_church). However, there are other present[^cite_note-386] and historical[^cite_note-387] Christian groups that do not fit neatly into one of these primary categories.

There is a diversity of [doctrines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctrine) and [liturgical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy) practices among groups calling themselves Christian. These groups may vary [ecclesiologically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiology) in their views on a classification of [Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination).[^cite_note-388] The Nicene Creed (325), however, is typically accepted as authoritative by most Christians, including the Catholic, Eastern Orthodox, Oriental Orthodox, and major Protestant, such as Lutheran and Anglican denominations.[^cite_note-389]
