### Catholic Church

Main article: [Catholic Church](606848.md)



[Pope Leo XIV](54520372.md), the leader of the Catholic Church since 2025

The Catholic Church consists of those [particular Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particular_Church), headed by bishops, in communion with the [pope](23056.md), the bishop of Rome, as its highest authority in matters of faith, morality, and church governance.[^cite_note-vatican_lumen_gentium-390][^cite_note-391] Like [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox), the Catholic Church, through [apostolic succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_succession), traces its origins to the Christian community founded by Jesus Christ.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393] Catholics maintain that the "[one, holy, catholic, and apostolic church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Marks_of_the_Church)" founded by Jesus [subsists fully](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsistit_in) in the Catholic Church, but also acknowledges other Christian churches and communities[^cite_note-vatican_lumen_gentium-390][^cite_note-394] and works towards [reconciliation](146656.md) among all Christians.[^cite_note-vatican_lumen_gentium-390] The Catholic faith is detailed in the *[Catechism of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church)*.[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396]

Of its [seven sacraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacraments_of_the_Catholic_Church), the [Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist_in_the_Catholic_Church) is the principal one, celebrated [liturgically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_liturgy) in the [Mass](5684685.md).[^cite_note-397] The church teaches that through [consecration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consecration#Eucharist) by a [priest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priesthood_\(Catholic_Church\)), the sacrificial [bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramental_bread) and [wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramental_wine) [become the body and blood of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transubstantiation). The [Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Mary) is [venerated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_Mary_in_the_Catholic_Church) in the Catholic Church as [Mother of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_of_God) and [Queen of Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_of_Heaven), honoured in [dogmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariology_of_the_Catholic_Church#Dogmatic_teachings) and [devotions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_devotions).[^cite_note-marian_dogmas-398] Its teaching includes [Divine Mercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Mercy), [sanctification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctification) through faith and [evangelization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelization) of [the Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gospel) as well as [Catholic social teaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_social_teaching), which emphasizes voluntary support for the sick, the poor, and the afflicted through the [corporal and spiritual works of mercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Works_of_mercy). The Catholic Church operates thousands of [Catholic schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_schools), [universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_higher_education), [hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_health_care), and orphanages around the world, and is the largest non-government provider of [education](1452366.md) and [health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_and_the_Catholic_Church) in the world.[^cite_note-399] Among its other social services are numerous charitable and humanitarian organizations.

[Canon law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_law_of_the_Catholic_Church) ([Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *jus canonicum*)[^cite_note-400] is the [system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_system) of [laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law) and [legal principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_law) made and enforced by the [hierarchical authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchy_of_the_Catholic_Church) of the Catholic Church to regulate its external organisation and government and to order and direct the activities of Catholics toward the mission of the church.[^cite_note-401] The canon law of the Latin Church was the first modern Western legal system,[^cite_note-402] and is the oldest continuously functioning legal system in the West.[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404] while the distinctive traditions of [Eastern Catholic canon law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_canon_law) govern the 23 Eastern Catholic [particular churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particular_church) *[sui iuris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sui_iuris).*

As the world's oldest and largest continuously functioning international institution,[^cite_note-405] it has played a prominent role in the history and development of [Western civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture).[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004Preface-408][^cite_note-409] The 2,834 [sees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_see)[^cite_note-410] are grouped into [24 particular autonomous Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sui_iuris#Catholic_ecclesiastical_use) (the largest of which being the [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church)), each with its own distinct traditions regarding the [liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy) and the administering of [sacraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacraments_of_the_Catholic_Church).[^cite_note-411] With more than 1.1 billion baptized members, the Catholic Church is the largest Christian [church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Church) and represents 50.1%[^cite_note-Global_Christianity-1] of all Christians as well as 16.7% of the [world's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_population).[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-Adherents-413][^cite_note-414] Catholics live all over the world through [missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_missions), [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaspora), and [conversions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Christianity).

### Eastern Orthodox Church

Main article: [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md)



[St. George's Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._George's_Cathedral,_Istanbul) in [Istanbul](3391396.md): It has been the seat of the [Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople) whose leader is regarded as the *[primus inter pares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primus_inter_pares)* in the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md).

[^cite_note-415]

The Eastern Orthodox Church consists of those churches in communion with the [patriarchal](175409.md) sees of the East, such as the [Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople).[^cite_note-416] Like the Catholic Church, the Eastern Orthodox Church also traces its heritage to the foundation of Christianity through [apostolic succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_succession) and has an [episcopal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_polity) structure, though the [autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephaly) of its component parts is emphasized, and most of them are national churches.

[Eastern Orthodox theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_theology) is based on [holy tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_tradition) which incorporates the dogmatic decrees of the [seven Ecumenical Councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_seven_ecumenical_councils), the Scriptures, and the teaching of the [Church Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Fathers). The church teaches that it is the [one, holy, catholic and apostolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Marks_of_the_Church) [church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_true_church) established by [Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Christianity) in his [Great Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Commission),[^cite_note-417] and that its bishops are the successors of Christ's apostles.[^cite_note-418] It maintains that it practises the original Christian faith, as passed down by holy tradition. Its [patriarchates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchates), reminiscent of the [pentarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentarchy), and other [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephalous) and [autonomous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous) churches reflect a variety of [hierarchical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchical) [organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church_organization). It recognizes seven major sacraments, of which the [Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist) is the principal one, celebrated [liturgically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Liturgy) in [synaxis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synaxis). The church teaches that through [consecration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consecration#Eucharist) [invoked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epiclesis) by a [priest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priesthood_\(Orthodox_Church\)), the sacrificial bread and wine become the body and blood of Christ. The [Virgin Mary](73513.md) is [venerated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration) in the Eastern Orthodox Church as the *[Theotokos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theotokos)*, meaning God-bearer, and is honoured in [devotions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_devotions#Eastern_Orthodoxy).

Eastern Orthodoxy is the second largest single denomination in Christianity, with an estimated 300 million adherents,[^cite_note-\:4-419] although [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) collectively outnumber them, substantially.[^cite_note-Global_Christianity-1][^cite_note-CSGC2019-420] As one of the oldest surviving religious institutions in the world, the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md) has played a prominent role in the history and culture of [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) and [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe), the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus), and the [Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_East).[^cite_note-421] The majority of Eastern Orthodox Christians live mainly in [Southeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Europe) and Eastern Europe, [Cyprus](5593.md), [Georgia](48768.md), and parts of the Caucasus region, [Siberia](29639.md), and the [Russian Far East](173135.md). Over half of Eastern Orthodox Christians follow the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church), while the vast majority live within [Russia](25391.md).[^cite_note-\:2-422] There are also communities in the former [Byzantine regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) of Africa, the [Eastern Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Mediterranean), and in the [Middle East](19323.md). Eastern Orthodox communities are also present in many other parts of the world, particularly North America, Western Europe, and Australia, formed through [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaspora), [conversions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Christianity), and [missionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionary) activity.[^cite_note-423]

### Oriental Orthodox Churches

Main article: [Oriental Orthodox Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches)



[Holy Trinity Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Trinity_Cathedral_\(Addis_Ababa\)) in [Addis Ababa](52593.md), the seat of the Ethiopian Orthodox; the largest of the Oriental Orthodox Churches

The [Oriental Orthodox Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches) (also called "Old Oriental" churches) are those eastern churches that recognize the first three ecumenical councils—[Nicaea](11118.md), [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Council_of_Constantinople), and [Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Council_of_Ephesus)—but reject the dogmatic definitions of the [Council of Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon) and instead espouse a [Miaphysite christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miaphysite_christology).

The Oriental Orthodox communion consists of six groups: [Syriac Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Orthodox), [Coptic Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church), [Ethiopian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Orthodox), [Eritrean Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eritrean_Orthodox), [Malankara Orthodox Syrian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malankara_Orthodox_Syrian_Church) (India), and [Armenian Apostolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic) churches.[^cite_note-424] These six churches, while being in communion with each other, are completely independent hierarchically.[^cite_note-425] These churches are generally not in communion with the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md), with whom they are in dialogue for erecting a communion.[^cite_note-sor.cua.edu-426] Together, they have about 62 million members worldwide.[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430][^cite_note-CSGC2019-420]

As some of the oldest religious institutions in the world, the Oriental Orthodox Churches have played a prominent role in the history and culture of [Armenia](10918072.md), [Egypt](8087628.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), [Eritrea](17238590.md), [Ethiopia](187749.md), [Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Nubia), [Iran](14653.md), [Azerbaijan](746.md),[^cite_note-431] and parts of the [Middle East](19323.md) and [India](14533.md).[^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434] An Eastern Christian body of [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephaly) [churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination), its [bishops](4092.md) are equal by virtue of [episcopal ordination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_ordination), and its doctrines can be summarized in that the churches recognize the validity of only the first three [ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_council).[^cite_note-435]

Some followers of Oriental Orthodox Churches follow cultural practices that are reminiscent of [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament) practices, like [abstaining from pork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_restrictions_on_the_consumption_of_pork),[^cite_note-436] [circumcising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumcising) male babies,[^cite_note-437] and observing [ritual purification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ritual_purification).[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439]

### Church of the East

Further information: [Assyrian Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Church_of_the_East) and [Ancient Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Church_of_the_East)



A 6th-century [Nestorian church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestorian_church), St. John the Arab, in the [Assyrian village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_tribes) of [Geramon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andac) in [Hakkari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakkari_\(historical_region\)), southeastern Turkey.

The [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East), which was part of the [Great Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Church),[^cite_note-440] shared [communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communion_\(Christian\)) with those in the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) until the [Council of Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ephesus) [condemned Nestorius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestorian_schism) in 431. Continuing as a *[dhimmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi)* community under the [Rashidun Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) after the [Muslim conquest of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia) (633–654), the Church of the East played a major role in the history of [Christianity in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Asia). Between the 9th and 14th centuries, it represented the world's largest [Christian denomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination) in terms of geographical extent. It established [dioceses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dioceses_of_the_Church_of_the_East_to_1318) and communities stretching from the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) and today's Iraq and [Iran](14653.md), to [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_\(East_Syriac_ecclesiastical_province\)) (the [Saint Thomas Syrian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Thomas_Christians) of [Kerala](4349459.md)), the [Mongol kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_among_the_Mongols) in Central Asia, and [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East_in_China) during the [Tang dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_dynasty) (7th–9th centuries). In the 13th and 14th centuries, the church experienced a final period of expansion under the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire), where influential Church of the East clergy sat in the Mongol court.

The [Assyrian Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Church_of_the_East), with an unbroken patriarchate established in the 17th century, is an independent [Eastern Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christian) denomination which claims continuity from the [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East)—in parallel to the Catholic patriarchate established in the 16th century that evolved into the [Chaldean Catholic Church](698821.md), an [Eastern Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Catholic) church in [full communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_communion) with the [Pope](23056.md). It is an Eastern Christian [church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination) that follows the traditional [christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology) and [ecclesiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiology) of the historical Church of the East. Largely [aniconic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aniconism_in_Christianity) and not in [communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communion_\(Christianity\)) with any other church, it belongs to the eastern branch of [Syriac Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Christianity), and uses the [East Syriac Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Syriac_Rite) in its [liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy).[^cite_note-441]



[Saint Mary Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Mary_Church,_Urmia); an ancient Assyrian church located in the city of [Urmia](615663.md), Iran

Its main spoken language is [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language), a dialect of [Eastern Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Aramaic), and the majority of its adherents are ethnic [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people), mostly living in [Iran](14653.md), [Iraq](7515928.md), [Syria](7515849.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), [India](14533.md) ([Chaldean Syrian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaldean_Syrian_Church)), and in the [Assyrian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_diaspora).[^cite_note-442] It is officially headquartered in the city of [Erbil](24534207.md) in northern [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md), and its original area also spreads into south-eastern Turkey and north-western Iran, corresponding to ancient [Assyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria). Its hierarchy is composed of [metropolitan bishops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_bishop) and [diocesan bishops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocesan_bishop), while lower clergy consists of [priests](23707.md) and [deacons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deacon), who serve in dioceses (eparchies) and parishes throughout the Middle East, India, North America, Oceania, and Europe (including the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus) and Russia).[^cite_note-443]

The [Ancient Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Church_of_the_East) distinguished itself from the [Assyrian Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Church_of_the_East) in 1964. It is one of the [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people) churches that claim continuity with the historical Church of the East, one of the oldest Christian churches in Mesopotamia.[^cite_note-444] It is officially headquartered in the city of [Baghdad](4492.md), Iraq.[^cite_note-blackwell1-445] The majority of its adherents are ethnic Assyrians.[^cite_note-blackwell1-445]

### Protestantism

Main articles: [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) and [Proto-Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Protestantism)

See also: [Protestant ecclesiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_ecclesiology)

In 1521, the [Edict of Worms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Worms) condemned [Martin Luther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther) and officially banned citizens of the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire) from defending or propagating his ideas.[^cite_note-446] This split within the Roman Catholic church is now called the [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation). Prominent Reformers included Martin Luther, [Huldrych Zwingli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huldrych_Zwingli), and [John Calvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Calvin). The 1529 [Protestation at Speyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestation_at_Speyer) against being excommunicated gave this party the name [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism). Luther's primary theological heirs are known as [Lutherans](23371382.md). Zwingli and Calvin's heirs are far broader denominationally and are referred to as the [Reformed tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinism).[^cite_note-447] The [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism) churches descended from the [Church of England](5955.md) and organized in the [Anglican Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Communion). Some Lutherans identify as [Evangelical Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Catholic) and some but not all Anglicans consider themselves both Protestant and Catholic.[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450] Protestants have developed [their own culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_culture), with major contributions in education, the [humanities and sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merton_thesis), the political and social order, the [economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_work_ethic) and the arts, and many other fields.[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452]

Since the Anglican, Lutheran, and the Reformed branches of Protestantism originated for the most part in cooperation with the government, these movements are termed the "[Magisterial Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magisterial_Reformation)". On the other hand, groups such as the [Anabaptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anabaptism), who often do not consider themselves to be Protestant, originated in the [Radical Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_Reformation), which though sometimes protected under *Acts of Toleration*, do not trace their history back to any state church. They are further distinguished by their rejection of infant baptism; they believe in baptism only of adult believers—[credobaptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credobaptism) (Anabaptists include the [Amish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amish), [Apostolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Christian_Church), [Bruderhof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruderhof), [Mennonites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mennonites), [Hutterites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutterites), [River Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Brethren) and [Schwarzenau Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwarzenau_Brethren) groups.)[^cite_note-453][^cite_note-454][^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456]

The term *Protestant* also refers to any churches which formed later, with either the Magisterial or Radical traditions. In the 18th century, for example, [Methodism](20119.md) grew out of [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican) minister [John Wesley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wesley)'s [evangelical revival movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Revival).[^cite_note-457] Several [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostal) and [non-denominational churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_Christianity), which emphasize the cleansing power of the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_in_Christianity), in turn grew out of Methodism.[^cite_note-458] Because Methodists, Pentecostals and other evangelicals stress "accepting Jesus as your personal Lord and Savior",[^cite_note-459] which comes from Wesley's emphasis of the [New Birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Born_again_\(Christianity\)),[^cite_note-460] they often refer to themselves as being [born again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Born_again).[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462]

Protestantism is the second largest major group of Christians after Catholicism by number of followers, although the Eastern Orthodox Church is larger than any single Protestant denomination.[^cite_note-Adherents-413] Estimates vary, mainly over the question of which denominations to classify as Protestant. The total Protestant population has reached 1.17 billion in 2024, corresponding to nearly 44% of the world's Christians.[^cite_note-gordonconwell.edu-317][^cite_note-CSGC2019-420][^cite_note-pewforum1-314][^cite_note-KimOsmerSchweitzer2018-318][^cite_note-463] The majority of Protestants are members of just a handful of denominational families, i.e. [Adventism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventism), [Anabaptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anabaptism) ([Amish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amish), [Apostolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Christian_Church), [Bruderhof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruderhof_Communities), [Hutterites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutterites), [Mennonites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mennonites), [River Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Brethren), and [Schwarzenau Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwarzenau_Brethren)), [Anglicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism), [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptist), [Lutheranism](23371382.md), [Methodism](20119.md), [Moravianism/Hussites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moravian_Church), [Pentecostalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism), [Plymouth Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plymouth_Brethren), [Quakerism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers), [Reformed Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinism) ([Congregationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_church), [Continental Reformed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Reformed_Protestantism), [Reformed Anglicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_Anglican), and [Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterianism)),[^cite_note-464] and [Waldensianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldensians) are the main families of Protestantism.[^cite_note-Melton2005-10][^cite_note-pewforum1-314] [Nondenominational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_Christianity), [evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism), [charismatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charismatic_Movement), [neo-charismatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-charismatic_churches), independent, and other churches are on the rise, and constitute a significant part of Protestant Christianity.[^cite_note-465]

Some groups of individuals who hold basic Protestant tenets identify themselves as "Christians" or "[born-again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Born-again) Christians". They typically distance themselves from the [confessionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confessionalism_\(religion\)) and [creedalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creed) of other Christian communities[^cite_note-ReferenceA-466] by calling themselves "[non-denominational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-denominational_Christianity)" or "[evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical)". Often founded by individual pastors, they have little affiliation with historic denominations.[^cite_note-467]

Historical chart of the main Protestant branches&#x20;

Historical chart of the main Protestant branches&#x20;

### Restorationism

Main article: [Restorationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restorationism)



A 19th-century drawing of [Joseph Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Smith) and [Oliver Cowdery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Cowdery) receiving the [Aaronic priesthood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaronic_priesthood_\(Latter_Day_Saints\)) from [John the Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Baptist). [Latter Day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latter_Day_Saint_movement) believe that the [Priesthood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_priest) ceased to exist on the earth after the death of the apostles and therefore needed to be [restored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_\(Latter_Day_Saints\)).

The [Second Great Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Great_Awakening), a period of religious revival that occurred in the United States during the early 1800s, saw the development of a number of unrelated churches. They generally saw themselves as [restoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restorationism) the original church of Jesus Christ rather than reforming one of the existing churches.[^cite_note-468] A common belief held by Restorationists was that the other divisions of Christianity had introduced doctrinal defects into Christianity, which was known as the [Great Apostasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Apostasy).[^cite_note-469] In Asia, [Iglesia ni Cristo](288343.md) is a known Restorationist denomination that was established during the early 1900s. Other examples of Restorationist denominations include [Irvingianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Apostolic_Church) and [Swedenborgianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedenborgians).[^cite_note-470][^cite_note-Spinks2017-13]

Some of the churches originating during this period are historically connected to early 19th-century camp meetings in the Midwest and upstate New York. One of the largest churches produced from the movement is [the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints).[^cite_note-471] American [Millennialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennialism) and [Adventism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventism), which arose from Evangelical Protestantism, influenced the [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses) movement and, as a reaction specifically to [William Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Miller_\(preacher\)), the [Seventh-day Adventists](28632.md). Others, including the [Christian Church (Disciples of Christ)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Church_\(Disciples_of_Christ\)), [Evangelical Christian Church in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Christian_Church_in_Canada),[^cite_note-472][^cite_note-473] [Churches of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churches_of_Christ), and the [Christian churches and churches of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_churches_and_churches_of_Christ), have their roots in the contemporaneous Stone-Campbell [Restoration Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_Movement), which was centered in Kentucky and Tennessee. Other groups originating in this time period include the [Christadelphians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christadelphians) and the previously mentioned [Latter Day Saints movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latter_Day_Saints_movement). While the churches originating in the Second Great Awakening have some superficial similarities, their doctrine and practices vary significantly.[^cite_note-474]

### Other



[Unitarian Church of Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarian_Church_of_Transylvania) in [Cluj-Napoca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cluj-Napoca)

Within Italy, Poland, Lithuania, Transylvania, Hungary, Romania, and the United Kingdom, [Unitarian Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarianism) emerged from the [Reformed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_tradition) tradition in the 16th century;[^cite_note-475] the [Unitarian Church of Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarian_Church_of_Transylvania) is an example of such a denomination that arose in this era.[^cite_note-476] They adopted the [Anabaptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anabaptist) doctrine of [credobaptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credobaptism).[^cite_note-477]

Various smaller [Independent Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Catholic) communities, such as the [Old Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Catholic_Church),[^cite_note-478] include the word *[Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_\(term\))* in their title, and arguably have more or less liturgical practices in common with the Catholic Church but are no longer in [full communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_communion) with the [Holy See](13393.md).[^cite_note-479]

[Spiritual Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_Christians), such as the [Doukhobors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doukhobors) and [Molokans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molokan), broke from the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) and maintain close association with Mennonites and [Quakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers) due to similar religious practices; all of these groups are furthermore collectively considered to be [peace churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_churches) due to their belief in [pacifism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_pacifism).[^cite_note-480][^cite_note-481]

[Messianic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messianic_Judaism) (or the Messianic Movement) is the name of a Christian movement comprising a number of streams, whose members may consider themselves Jewish. The movement originated in the 1960s and 1970s, and it blends elements of religious Jewish practice with evangelical Christianity. Messianic Judaism affirms Christian creeds such as the messiahship and divinity of "Yeshua" (the Hebrew name of Jesus) and the Triune Nature of God, while also adhering to some Jewish dietary laws and customs.[^cite_note-482]

[Esoteric Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esoteric_Christianity), such as [The Christian Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Christian_Community),[^cite_note-483] regard Christianity as a [mystery religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_esotericism)[^cite_note-484][^cite_note-485] and profess the existence and possession of certain esoteric doctrines or practices,[^cite_note-486][^cite_note-487] hidden from the public and accessible only to a narrow circle of "enlightened", "initiated", or highly educated people.[^cite_note-488][^cite_note-489]

[Nondenominational Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_Christianity) or non-denominational Christianity consists of [churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simple_church) which typically distance themselves from the [confessionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confessionalism_\(religion\)) or [creedalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creed) of other Christian communities[^cite_note-ReferenceA-466] by not formally aligning with a specific [Christian denomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination).[^cite_note-Hughes1997-490] Nondenominational Christianity first arose in the 18th century through the [Stone-Campbell Restoration Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone-Campbell_Restoration_Movement), with followers organizing themselves as "[Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christians_\(Stone_Movement\))" and "[Disciples of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disciples_of_Christ_\(Campbell_Movement\))",[^cite_note-492][^cite_note-Hughes1997-490][^cite_note-Barnett2020-491] but many typically adhere to [evangelical Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Christianity).[^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494][^cite_note-495]

## Cultural influence

Main articles: [Christian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_culture) and [Role of Christianity in civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Role_of_Christianity_in_civilization)

Further information: [Protestant culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_culture) and [Christian influences in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_influences_in_Islam)

[Christian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_culture)











*Clockwise from top*: [Sistine Chapel ceiling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistine_Chapel_ceiling), [Notre-Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre-Dame_de_Paris) cathedral in Paris, Eastern Orthodox [wedding](32893.md), *[Christ the Redeemer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_the_Redeemer_\(statue\))* statue, [Nativity scene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativity_scene)

The history of the [Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom) spans about 1,700 years and includes a variety of socio-political developments, as well as advances in the [arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_art), [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_cathedrals_and_great_churches), [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_literature), [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_and_science), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_philosophy), and technology.[^cite_note-Crisis_in_Western_Education-496][^cite_note-497][^cite_note-498] Since the spread of Christianity from the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) to [Europe](9239.md) and [North Africa](21714.md) during the early [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), Christendom has been divided in the pre-existing [Greek East and Latin West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_East_and_Latin_West). Consequently, different versions of the Christian cultures arose with their own rites and practices, centered around the cities of Rome ([Western Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity)) and [Carthage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Carthage), whose communities were called Western or Latin Christendom,[^cite_note-499] and [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) ([Eastern Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity)), [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioch) ([Syriac Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Christianity)), [Kerala](4349459.md) ([Indian Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Thomas_Christians)) and [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) ([Coptic Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Christianity)), whose communities were called Eastern or Oriental Christendom.[^cite_note-500][^cite_note-501][^cite_note-502] The [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) was one of the peaks in [Christian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_history) and Eastern [Christian civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_civilization).[^cite_note-Cameron_2006_42-54] From the 11th to 13th centuries, [Latin Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Christendom) rose to the central role of the [Western world](21208200.md).

The Bible has had a profound influence on Western civilization and on cultures around the globe; it has contributed to the formation of [Western law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_law), [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_art), [texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_literature), and education.[^cite_note-503][^cite_note-504][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiches2000ch._1-505] With a literary tradition spanning two millennia, the Bible is one of the most influential works ever written. From practices of [personal hygiene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hygiene_in_Christianity) to philosophy and ethics, the Bible has directly and indirectly influenced politics and law, war and peace, sexual morals, marriage and family life, toilet etiquette, letters and learning, the arts, economics, social justice, medical care and more.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiches2000ch._1-505]

[Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_Christians) have made a myriad of contributions to [human progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress_\(history\)) in a broad and diverse range of fields, including philosophy,[^cite_note-506] [science and technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christians_in_science_and_technology),[^cite_note-507][^cite_note-508][^cite_note-509][^cite_note-510] [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_health_care),[^cite_note-511] [fine arts and architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Catholic_Church_artists),[^cite_note-512] [politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_and_politics), [literatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Catholic_authors), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_music),[^cite_note-513] and business.[^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515] According to *100 Years of Nobel Prizes* a review of the Nobel Prizes award between 1901 and 2000 reveals that (65.4%) of [Nobel Prizes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobel_Prizes) Laureates, [have identified Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_Nobel_laureates) in its various forms as their religious preference.[^cite_note-516]

Outside the Western world, Christianity has had an influence on various cultures, such as in Africa, the Near East, Middle East, East Asia, Southeast Asia, and the Indian subcontinent.[^cite_note-Curtis_2017_173-517][^cite_note-518] [Eastern Christian scientists and scholars of the medieval Islamic world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_scientists_and_scholars_of_the_medieval_Islamic_world) (particularly [Jacobite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Orthodox_Church) and [Nestorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestorianism) Christians) contributed to the [Islamic civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age) during the reign of the [Umayyads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad) and the [Abbasids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid), by translating works of [Greek philosophers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_philosophers) to [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language) and afterwards, to [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language).[^cite_note-519][^cite_note-520][^cite_note-521] They also excelled in philosophy, science, theology, and medicine.[^cite_note-522][^cite_note-523][^cite_note-524] Scholars and intellectuals agree [Christians in the Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christians_in_the_Middle_East) have made significant contributions to Islamic civilization since the introduction of [Islam](6037917.md), and they have had a significant impact contributing the culture of the [Mashriq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mashriq), [Turkey](11125639.md), and [Iran](14653.md).[^cite_note-525][^cite_note-Curtis_2017_173-517]

### Influence on Western culture



[Thomas Aquinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas), an Italian [Dominican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Order) [friar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friar) and [priest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_priest), the foremost [Scholastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholasticism) thinker,

[^cite_note-526]

&#x20;as well as one of the most influential philosophers and theologians in the Western tradition.

[^cite_note-527]

[Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture), throughout most of its history, has been nearly equivalent to [Christian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_culture), and a large portion of the population of the Western Hemisphere can be described as practicing or nominal Christians. The notion of "Europe" and the "Western World" has been intimately connected with the concept of "Christianity and Christendom". Many historians even attribute Christianity for being the link that created a unified [European identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_identity).[^cite_note-528]

Though Western culture contained several polytheistic religions during its early years under the [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) and [Roman Empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), as the centralized Roman power waned, the dominance of the Catholic Church was the only consistent force in Western Europe.[^cite_note-Koch-1994-529] Until the [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment),[^cite_note-Koch-1994-529] Christian culture guided the course of philosophy, literature, art, music and science.[^cite_note-Koch-1994-529][^cite_note-Crisis_in_Western_Education-496] Christian disciplines of the respective arts have subsequently developed into [Christian philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_philosophy), [Christian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_art), [Christian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_music), [Christian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_literature), and so on.

Christianity has had a significant impact on education, as the church created the bases of the Western system of education,[^cite_note-Britannica2022-530] and was the sponsor of [founding universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_university) in the Western world, as the university is generally regarded as an institution that has its origin in the [Medieval Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity) setting.[^cite_note-verger1999-74] Historically, Christianity has often been a patron of science and medicine; many [Catholic clergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Catholic_cleric-scientists),[^cite_note-531] [Jesuits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jesuit_scientists) in particular,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoods2005109-532][^cite_note-533][^cite_note-534] have been active in the sciences throughout history and have made significant contributions to the [development of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_and_science).[^cite_note-535][^cite_note-536] Some scholars state that Christianity contributed to the rise of the [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution).[^cite_note-537] Protestantism also has had an important influence on science. According to the [Merton Thesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merton_Thesis), there was a positive correlation between the rise of English [Puritanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puritanism) and German [Pietism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pietism) on the one hand, and early experimental science on the other.[^cite_note-538][^cite_note-539][^cite_note-540] The civilizing influence of Christianity includes social welfare,[^cite_note-Britannica2022-530] contribution to the medical and health care,[^cite_note-541] founding hospitals,[^cite_note-Britannica2022-530] economics (as the [Protestant work ethic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_work_ethic)),[^cite_note-Britannica2022-530][^cite_note-542][^cite_note-543] architecture,[^cite_note-544] literature,[^cite_note-545] [personal hygiene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hygiene_in_Christianity) ([ablution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ablution_in_Christianity)),[^cite_note-546][^cite_note-547] and family life.[^cite_note-Britannica2022-530][^cite_note-548] Historically, *[extended families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extended_family)* were the basic family unit in the [Christian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_culture) and [countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_countries).[^cite_note-549]

[Cultural Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Christian) are secular people with a Christian heritage who may not believe in the religious claims of Christianity, but who retain an affinity for the popular culture, art, [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_music), and so on related to the religion.[^cite_note-550]

*[Postchristianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postchristianity)* is a term for the [decline of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_Christianity), particularly in [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Europe), [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Canada), [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Australia), and to a minor degree the [Southern Cone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cone), in the 20th and 21st centuries, considered in terms of [postmodernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodernism). It refers to the loss of Christianity's monopoly on [values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Values) and [world view](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_view) in historically Christian societies.[^cite_note-551]

Christian leaders and congregations have had an influence on the broader [environmental movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_movement). Christian teachings relate to ecology, and draw on teachings of the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) to promote the moral responsibility of humans to care for God's creation. Christianity acknowledges the tension between humanity's duty to care for God's creation and the natural human inclination to resist God's will.[^cite_note-552] In recent decades, major Christian denominations have revisited their teachings and practices in response to the environmental crisis. There has been a rise in activity in Christian congregations to curb [climate change](5042951.md),[^cite_note-553] partly inspired by the advocacy of [Pope Francis](1687680.md) following his publication of the encyclical letter [Laudato Si'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laudato_Si')—On Care for Our Common Home. Organizations such as Green Christian,[^cite_note-554] [Laudato Si' Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laudato_Si'_Movement),[^cite_note-555] and the [Interfaith Center for Sustainable Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_Center_for_Sustainable_Development)[^cite_note-556] are a few examples of groups working in the Christian and ecology movement. These initiatives reflect how Christians, alongside other people of faith, are increasingly embracing ecological concerns, recognizing that the protection of the [Earth](9228.md) is a spiritual imperative tied to faith and justice.[^cite_note-557]

## Ecumenism

Main article: [Ecumenism](146656.md)



Bishop [John M. Quinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_M._Quinn) of the [Roman Catholic Diocese of Winona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Winona) and Bishop Steven Delzer of [Evangelical Lutheran Southeastern Minnesota Synod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ELCA_synods) leading a [Reformation Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation_Day) service (2017)

Christian groups, sects and [denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_denominations) have long expressed ideals of being reconciled, and in the 20th century, Christian ecumenism advanced in two ways.[^cite_note-\:2-422][^cite_note-McManners581-558][^cite_note-559] One way was greater cooperation between groups, such as the [World Evangelical Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Evangelical_Alliance) founded in 1846 in London or the [Edinburgh Missionary Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Missionary_Conference) of Protestants in 1910, the Justice, Peace and Creation Commission of the [World Council of Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Council_of_Churches) founded in 1948 by Protestant and Orthodox churches, and similar national councils like the [National Council of Churches in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_of_Churches_in_Australia), which includes Catholics.[^cite_note-McManners581-558]

The other way was an institutional union with [united churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_and_uniting_churches), a practice that can be traced back to unions between Lutherans and Calvinists in early 19th-century Germany. Congregationalist, Methodist, and Presbyterian churches united in 1925 to form the [United Church of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Church_of_Canada),[^cite_note-560] and in 1977 to form the [Uniting Church in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniting_Church_in_Australia). The [Church of South India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_South_India) was formed in 1947 by the union of Anglican, Baptist, Methodist, Congregationalist, and Presbyterian churches.[^cite_note-561]

The [Christian Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Flag) is an ecumenical flag designed in the early 20th century to represent all of Christianity and [Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom).[^cite_note-562] Substantial agreement between various Christian denominations, especially those of Catholicism and Protestantism, has led to a unified presentation of the Christian religion in *[The Common Catechism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Common_Catechism)*.[^cite_note-Bent1994-563][^cite_note-Phan2016-564]

The ecumenical, [monastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_monasticism) [Taizé Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiz%C3%A9_Community) is notable for being composed of more than one hundred [brothers](419369.md) from Protestant and Catholic traditions.[^cite_note-taize-565] The community emphasizes the reconciliation of all denominations and its main church, located in [Taizé, Saône-et-Loire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiz%C3%A9,_Sa%C3%B4ne-et-Loire), France, is named the "Church of Reconciliation".[^cite_note-taize-565] The community is internationally known, attracting over 100,000 young [pilgrims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage) annually.[^cite_note-566]

Steps towards reconciliation on a global level were taken in 1965 by the Catholic and Orthodox churches, mutually revoking the excommunications that marked their [Great Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East-West_Schism) in 1054;[^cite_note-567] the Anglican Catholic International Commission (ARCIC) working towards full communion between those churches since 1970;[^cite_note-568] and the [Lutheran World Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutheran_World_Federation) and the Catholic Church signing the [Joint Declaration on the Doctrine of Justification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Declaration_on_the_Doctrine_of_Justification) in 1999 to address conflicts at the root of the Protestant Reformation. In 2006, the [World Methodist Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Methodist_Council), representing all Methodist denominations, adopted the declaration.[^cite_note-569]

## Criticism, persecution, and apologetics

Main articles: [Criticism of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Christianity), [Persecution of Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians), and [Christian apologetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_apologetics)

See also: [Anti-Christian sentiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Christian_sentiment)

### Criticism



The monument to [Giordano Bruno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giordano_Bruno) in the place he was executed in Rome

Criticism of Christianity and Christians goes back to the [Apostolic Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Age), with the New Testament recording friction between the followers of Jesus and the [Pharisees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharisees) and [scribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scribes) (e.g., [Matthew 15\:1–20](https://www.biblica.com/bible/?osis=niv\:Matthew%2015\:1%E2%80%9320) and [Mark 7\:1–23](https://www.biblica.com/bible/?osis=niv\:Mark%207\:1%E2%80%9323)).[^cite_note-570] In the 2nd century, Christianity was criticized by the Jews on various grounds, e.g., that the prophecies of the Hebrew Bible could not have been fulfilled by Jesus, given that he did not have a successful life.[^cite_note-571] Additionally, a sacrifice to remove sins in advance, for everyone or as a human being, did not fit the Jewish sacrifice ritual; furthermore, [God in Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism) is said to judge people on their deeds instead of their beliefs.[^cite_note-572][^cite_note-573] One of the first comprehensive attacks on Christianity came from the Greek philosopher [Celsus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celsus), who wrote *[The True Word](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_True_Word)*, a polemic criticizing Christians as being unprofitable members of society.[^cite_note-574][^cite_note-575][^cite_note-Olson1999-576] In response, the church father [Origen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origen) published his treatise *[Contra Celsum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contra_Celsum)*, or *Against Celsus*, a seminal work of Christian apologetics, which systematically addressed Celsus's criticisms and helped bring Christianity a level of academic respectability.[^cite_note-577][^cite_note-Olson1999-576]

By the 3rd century, criticism of Christianity had mounted. Wild rumors about Christians were widely circulated, claiming that they were [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) and that, as part of their rituals, they devoured human infants and engaged in incestuous orgies.[^cite_note-578][^cite_note-579] The [Neoplatonist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoplatonism) philosopher [Porphyry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porphyry_\(philosopher\)) wrote the fifteen-volume *Adversus Christianos* as a comprehensive attack on Christianity, in part building on the teachings of [Plotinus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plotinus).[^cite_note-580][^cite_note-581]

By the 12th century, the [Mishneh Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishneh_Torah) (i.e., [Rabbi](51273.md) [Moses Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_Maimonides)) was criticizing Christianity on the grounds of idol worship, in that Christians attributed divinity to Jesus, who had a physical body.[^cite_note-582] In the 19th century, [Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nietzsche) began to write a series of polemics on the "unnatural" teachings of Christianity (e.g. sexual abstinence), and continued his criticism of Christianity to the end of his life.[^cite_note-583] In the 20th century, the philosopher [Bertrand Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertrand_Russell) expressed his criticism of Christianity in *[Why I Am Not a Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Why_I_Am_Not_a_Christian)*, formulating his rejection of Christianity.[^cite_note-584]

Criticism of Christianity continues to date, e.g. [Jewish](25955086.md) and [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) theologians criticize the doctrine of the [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity) held by most Christians, stating that this doctrine in effect assumes that there are three gods, running against the basic tenet of [monotheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism).[^cite_note-585] New Testament scholar [Robert M. Price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_M._Price) has outlined the possibility that some Bible stories are based partly on myth in *The Christ Myth Theory and its problems*.[^cite_note-586]

### Persecution



Christians fleeing their homes in the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), c. 1922. Many Christians were persecuted and killed during the [Armenian genocide](140376.md), [Greek genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide), and [Assyrian genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_genocide).

[^cite_note-587]

Christians are one of the most [persecuted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians) religious groups in the world, especially in the [Middle-East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_Middle_East), [North Africa](21714.md) and South and East Asia.[^cite_note-588][^cite_note-589][^cite_note-Wintour_2019_Guardian-590] In 2017, [Open Doors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_Doors) estimated approximately 260 million Christians are subjected annually to "high, very high, or extreme persecution"[^cite_note-591] with North Korea considered the most hazardous nation for Christians.[^cite_note-592][^cite_note-593] In 2019, a report[^cite_note-594][^cite_note-Mounstephen_2019_FCO_final_rep-595] commissioned by the United Kingdom's [Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_and_Commonwealth_Affairs) of the [Foreign and Commonwealth Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_and_Commonwealth_Office) (FCO) to investigate global persecution of Christians found persecution has increased, and is highest in the Middle East, North Africa, India, China, North Korea, and Latin America, among others, and that it is global and not limited to Islamic states.[^cite_note-596][^cite_note-Mounstephen_2019_FCO_final_rep-595] This investigation found that approximately 80% of persecuted believers worldwide are Christians.[^cite_note-Wintour_2019_Guardian-590]

### Apologetics



A copy of the *[Summa Theologica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summa_Theologica)* by [Thomas Aquinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas)

Christian apologetics aims to present a [rational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reason) basis for Christianity. The word "apologetic" (Greek: ἀπολογητικός *apologētikos*) comes from the Greek verb ἀπολογέομαι *apologeomai*, meaning "(I) speak in defense of".[^cite_note-597] Christian apologetics has taken many forms over the centuries, starting with Paul the Apostle. The philosopher [Thomas Aquinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas) presented five arguments for God's existence in the *[Summa Theologica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summa_Theologica)*, while his *[Summa contra Gentiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summa_contra_Gentiles)* was a major apologetic work.[^cite_note-598][^cite_note-599] Another famous apologist, [G. K. Chesterton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G._K._Chesterton), wrote in the early twentieth century about the benefits of religion and, specifically, Christianity. Famous for his use of paradox, Chesterton explained that while Christianity had the most mysteries, it was the most practical religion.[^cite_note-600][^cite_note-601] He pointed to the [advance of Christian civilizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Role_of_the_Christian_Church_in_civilization) as proof of its practicality.[^cite_note-602] The physicist and priest [John Polkinghorne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Polkinghorne), in his *[Questions of Truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Questions_of_Truth)*, discusses the subject of [religion and science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_and_science), a topic that other Christian apologists such as [Ravi Zacharias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravi_Zacharias), [John Lennox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lennox), and [William Lane Craig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Lane_Craig) have engaged, with the latter two men opining that the [inflationary Big Bang model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_interpretations_of_the_Big_Bang_theory) is evidence for the [existence of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Existence_of_God).[^cite_note-603] [Creationist apologetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creationist_apologetics) are apologetics that aim to defend [creationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creationism).

[^cite_note-11]: &#x20;The denominations of [Adventism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventism), [Anabaptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anabaptism) ([Amish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amish), [Apostolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Christian_Church), [Bruderhof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruderhof_Communities), [Hutterites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutterites), [Mennonites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mennonites), [River Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Brethren), and [Schwarzenau Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwarzenau_Brethren)), [Anglicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism), [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptist), [Lutheranism](23371382.md), [Methodism](20119.md), [Moravianism/Hussites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moravian_Church), [Pentecostalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism), [Plymouth Brethren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plymouth_Brethren), [Quakerism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers), [Reformed Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinism) ([Congregationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_church), [Continental Reformed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Reformed_Protestantism), and [Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterianism)), and [Waldensianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldensians) are the main families of Protestantism. Other groups that are sometimes regarded as Protestant include [non-denominational Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_Christianity) congregations.<sup>[\[8\]](#cite_note-Melton2005-10)</sup>

[^cite_note-14]: &#x20;The denominations of Restorationism include the [Irvingians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Apostolic_Church), [Swedenborgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Church_\(Swedenborgian\)), [Christadelphians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christadelphians), [Latter Day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latter_Day_Saint_movement), [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses), [La Luz del Mundo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Luz_del_Mundo), and [Iglesia ni Cristo](288343.md).<sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-12)</sup><sup>[\[10\]](#cite_note-Spinks2017-13)</sup>

[^cite_note-22]: &#x20;It appears in the Acts of the Apostles, [Acts 9\:2](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(King%20James\)/Acts#9\:2), [Acts 19\:9](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(King%20James\)/Acts#19\:9) and [Acts 19\:23](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(King%20James\)/Acts#19\:23). Some [English translations of the New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_translations_of_the_Bible) capitalize 'the Way' (e.g. the [New King James Version](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_King_James_Version) and the [English Standard Version](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Standard_Version)), indicating that this was how 'the new religion seemed then to be designated'<sup>[\[13\]](#cite_note-17)</sup> whereas others treat the phrase as indicative—'the way',<sup>[\[14\]](#cite_note-18)</sup> 'that way'<sup>[\[15\]](#cite_note-19)</sup> or 'the way of the Lord'.<sup>[\[16\]](#cite_note-20)</sup> The [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language) version reads, "the way of God" and the [Vulgate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulgate) Latin version, "the way of the Lord".<sup>[\[17\]](#cite_note-21)</sup>

[^cite_note-32]: &#x20;Many scholars consider the Parousia in the gospels to be contingent, though with detractors.<sup>[\[25\]](#cite_note-30)</sup> The timing of the end in early Christian expectation is also debated.<sup>[\[26\]](#cite_note-31)</sup>

[^cite_note-Trias-Trinitas-184]: &#x20;The Latin equivalent, from which English *trinity* is derived,<sup>[\[175\]](#cite_note-181)</sup> is *trinitas*<sup>[\[176\]](#cite_note-Trinitas-182)</sup> though Latin also borrowed Greek *trias* verbatim.<sup>[\[177\]](#cite_note-183)</sup>

[^cite_note-209]: &#x20;Often these are arranged on an [annual cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_cycle), using a book called a [lectionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lectionary).

The Second Great Awakening (1790-1840) spurred a renewed interest in primitive Christianity. What is known as the Restoration Movement of the nineteenth century gave birth to an array of groups: Mormons (The Latter Day Saint Movement), the Churches of Christ, Adventists, and Jehovah's Witnesses. Though these groups demonstrate a breathtaking diversity on the continuum of Christianity they share an intense restorationist impulse.

The Conservative Mennonite Conference practices believer's baptism, seen as an external symbol of internal spiritual purity and performed by immersion or pouring of water on the head; Communion; washing the feet of the saints, following Jesus' example and reminding believers of the need to be washed of pride, rivalry, and selfish motives; anointing the sick with oil – a symbol of the Holy Spirit and of the healing power of God—offered with the prayer of faith; and laying on of hands for ordination, symbolizing the imparting of responsibility and of God's power to fulfill that responsibility.

[^cite_note-252]: &#x20;Jordan, Anne (2000). *Christianity*. Nelson Thornes. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-7487-5320-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-7487-5320-8). When he was standing on a hillside, Jesus explained to his followers how they were to behave as God would wish. The talk has become known as the Sermon on the Mount, and is found in the Gospel of Matthew, chapter 5, 6 and 7. During the talk Jesus taught his followers how to pray and he gave them an example of suitable prayer. Christians call the prayer the Lord's Prayer, because it was taught by the Lord, Jesus Christ. It is also known as the Pattern Prayer as it provides a pattern for Christians to follow in prayer, to ensure that they pray in the way God and Jesus would want.

[^cite_note-253]: &#x20;Milavec, Aaron (2003). *The Didache: Faith, Hope, & Life of the Earliest Christian Communities, 50–70 C.E*. Paulist. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-8091-0537-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-8091-0537-3). Given the placement of the Lord's Prayer in the Didache, it was to be expected that the new member of the community would come to learn and to pray the Lord's Prayer at the appointed hours three times each day only after baptism (8\:2f.).

[^cite_note-254]: &#x20;[Beckwith, Roger T.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_T._Beckwith) (2005). *Calendar, Chronology And Worship: Studies in Ancient Judaism And Early Christianity*. Brill. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-90-04-14603-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-90-04-14603-7). So three minor hours of prayer were developed, at the third, sixth and ninth hours, which, as Dugmore points out, were ordinary divisions of the day for worldly affairs, and the Lord's Prayer was transferred to those hours.

[^cite_note-255]: &#x20;[Chadwick, Henry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Chadwick_\(theologian\)) (1993). *The Early Church*. Penguin. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-101-16042-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-101-16042-8). Hippolytus in the *Apostolic Tradition* directed that Christians should pray seven times a day – on rising, at the lighting of the evening lamp, at bedtime, at midnight, and also, if at home, at the third, sixth and ninth hours of the day, being hours associated with Christ's Passion. Prayers at the third, sixth, and ninth hours are similarly mentioned by Tertullian, Cyprian, Clement of Alexandria and Origen, and must have been very widely practised. These prayers were commonly associated with private Bible reading in the family.

[^cite_note-256]: &#x20;Lössl, Josef (2010). *The Early Church: History and Memory*. A\&C Black. p. 135. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-567-16561-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-567-16561-9). Not only the content of early Christian prayer was rooted in Jewish tradition; its daily structure too initially followed a Jewish pattern, with prayer times in the early morning, at noon and in the evening. Later (in the course of the second century), this pattern combined with another one; namely prayer times in the evening, at midnight and in the morning. As a result seven 'hours of prayer' emerged, which later became the monastic 'hours' and are still treated as 'standard' prayer times in many churches today. They are roughly equivalent to midnight, 6 a.m., 9 a.m., noon, 3 p.m., 6 p.m. and 9 p.m. Prayer positions included prostration, kneeling and standing. ... Crosses made of wood or stone, or painted on walls or laid out as mosaics, were also in use, at first not directly as objections of veneration but in order to 'orientate' the direction of prayer (i.e. towards the east, Latin *oriens*).

[Diocese of South-West America of the Malankara Orthodox Syrian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malankara_Orthodox_Diocese_of_Southwest_America). [^cite_note-WELS2014-291]: &#x20;["Methodist Beliefs: In what ways are Lutherans different from United Methodists?"](https://www.wels.net/what-we-believe/questions-answers/christian/methodist-beliefs). Wisconsin Evangelical Lutheran Synod. 2014. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20140522105449/https://www.wels.net/what-we-believe/questions-answers/christian/methodist-beliefs) from the original on 22 May 2014. Retrieved 22 May 2014. The United Methodists see Scripture as the primary source and criterion for Christian doctrine. They emphasize the importance of tradition, experience, and reason for Christian doctrine. Lutherans teach that the Bible is the sole source for Christian doctrine. The truths of Scripture do not need to be authenticated by tradition, human experience, or reason. Scripture is self authenticating and is true in and of itself.
